Issued 3 November 1892.
NOTEs: The Golden Legend, a mediaeval collection of saints' lives, held great appeal for Morris.
As Ellis succinctly phrased it in his ‘Memoranda': Among the books which serve to illustrate the religious life and mode of thought that prevailed in the middle ages, none holds a more important place than the “Legend Aurea."Compiled and put into form in the latter half of the 13th century, the welcome with which it was received by the clergy & the lettered world is attested by the great number of manuscripts of it which are still extant, while its ready reception by the people generally is evidenced by the fact that within a short time of its first appearance it was translated into the vulgar tongue of most of the nations of Europe, frequently with the hagiological preferences of the people of the country for which it was designed' (iii, 1282).
It was intended to be thc earliest book produced at the KP, but because of its length and some problems with the first delivery of paper from Batchelor (see Ai), Morris was forced to issue several shorter volumes before it was completed.
On 2ı July 189o, while the Golden type was still being cut, Morris purchased (from Sotheran, for $\mathcal { E } \mathfrak { s o } )$ (Sotheran to Cockerell, 30 November 1897 [BL C.102.h.18]) Wynkyn de Worde's 1527 reprint of Caxton's translation (now in NL); when Cockerell visited Kelmscott House on 14 August of that year, Morris read passages of it aloud to him, and Cockerell noted that ‘he thinks of reprinting [it] when his new type is all cast' (Cockerell diary).
But Morris was uncertain whether to proceed with The Golden Legend or Caxton's History of Troy until Bernard Quaritch lent him a copy of the latter, which persuaded him that the G.L. is by far the most important book of the two; so’– he wrote Ellis on 29 August 189o – I accept your kind offer with many thanks indeed, and will begin printing as soon as the type is free from the Glittering Plain' (Mackail, ii, 262).
In early September Ellis, Morris, and Quaritch were negotiating a contract (see Ei).
Quaritch was eager to publish the book, because, he said, first I think every man of culture should be acquainted with the alleged lives of the Saints, and second I am aware of the philological value of all books Caxton' (Quaritch to Ellis, 8 September 189o [Bc]).
Quaritch agreed to print 25o copies (a number almost immediately doubled) at his own expense, and Morris and Ellis were to contribute their services gratis in return for twelve copies each.
But the contract, which is dated 1 September, stipulated that Morris was ‘to have absolute and sole control over choice of paper, choice of type, size of the reprint and selection of the printer'.
Quaritch assumed that he would supply the paper, an idea that was abandoned after Morris met Joseph Batchelor later in the year.
Quaritch was startled to discover that the only other modern reprint of The Golden Legend was an incomplete facsimile published by the Holbein Society in 1878 (which Morris owned [Library, no.
484]); however, the Early English Text Society had published several other Caxton titles during the 187os and 188os, and David Nutt, the London publisher, was issuing a limited edition of Caxton's Le Morte Darthur between 1889 and 1891.
(For an account of the earlier English editions of The Golden Legend, see Pierce Butler, Legenda Aurea -Légende Dorée – Golden Legend: A Study of Caxton's Golden Legend . . . [Baltimore, 1899].) Since Morris did not own the Caxton edition (1483) of The Golden Legend (though he possessed at least ten other early editions by the time of his death), Ellis arranged to borrow it from the Cambridge University Library; his daughter Phyllis (afterwards Mrs.
Payne) then transcribed it so that the kP printers would not have to handle the book itself; and later Ellis read proofs against the book.
(W.
H.
Bowden reported that not one word of the transcription was illegible [Colebrook, p.
37]; and the surviving scraps of it [see a below] confirm this claim.) As usual, Morris and Ellis disagreed about how much editorial apparatus was needed: Ellis persuaded Edward Gordon Duff, the bibliographer, to prepare a list of English-language editions of The Golden Legend, but in the end Morris declined to include it. ‘The author-printer kicks much against introductions, and there is some practical difficulty as to bibliographical description in that there are but two sets of type[:] caps. for headings & that used for the text, Ellis explained to Duff on 20 March 1892 (HEH DF 268).
Meanwhile, Morris wrestled with problems of design and typography: his initial view was that the book would have to be ‘some kind of folio', and he argued that Caxton's joined letters and contractions should not be reproduced, ‘since our book is to be a reprint, not a facsimile' (Morris to Ellis, 14 September 189o [Mackail, ii, 263–4]).
(Morris also extensively altered Caxton's punctuation.) On 31 December he wrote to Ellis that the book could not be double column, since roman type is wider than blackletter (Mackail, ii, 26s-6).
By March 1891 Morris was experimenting with trial pages (see k, l, and m below), on which he made endless small adjustments in order to secure the correct proportions of margins.
Writing to Quaritch on 25 July 1891, Morris predicted that printing and binding would cost between £12oo and £14oo, and added: ‘Mr.
Burne Jones has promised to design me a frontispiece for the book and for my part I feel sure there will be a moderate public who will like it very much'(BQ).
Morris designed the border for page I and several of the initials while convalescing at Folkestone in April 1891 (Mackail, ii, 27o).
The engraved title was cut by Hooper (invoice: HEH HM 366s4), as were most of the initials and borders, though a few of the earliest initials were cut by George Campfield.
The first sheet was printed on 11 May 1891; the first volume, except for prelims and illustrations, was completed by October 1891.
In September 1891 Burne-Jones saw some proofs and reported that hewent crazy with pleasure'(Memorials, ii, 217). ‘The two illustrations and the title (which was the first woodcut title designed by Morris) were not engraved until June and August, 1892, when the third volume was approaching completion'(List’).
Both illustrations were touched up for the wood-engraver by Mr.
Fairfax Murray in a photographic copy' (Mackail, ii, 293).
In Morris's design for the engraved title (see d below), the vine ornament behind the lettering is much lighter than in the final version, and the last line reads ‘newly done'rather than ‘done anew'.
Cockerell reports that about half a dozen'vellum proofs of the illustrations were printed (‘List'; see also q, r, and t below).
Morris abandoned his plan to design a printer's mark incorporating a drawing of Kelmscott Manor (Mackail, ii, 293).
The books were delivered to Quaritch from the binder by 4 October 1892 (Quaritch to B.
Alfred Quaritch [Bod.
Ms.
Eng. lett. c.
435, fol.
179]).
Three days later Morris wrote to Quaritch: I must needs say that, looking at the finished book, I am proud of it, and of having pushed it through so promptly.
It is just 2 years ago that Ellis & I began talking about doing it in this very house [Kelmscott Manor] where I am writing' (BQ).
A receipt of the same date (Bod.
Ms.
Eng. lett. c.
436, fol.
184) reveals that the total cost of printing and binding was £135o – within Morris's original estimate.
The book was very favourably reviewed in The Times on 6 October (p.
4) and in the Library, 4 (1892), 346–7.
On 28 October 1892, at the Blue Posts Restaurant, Cork Street, there was a dinner to celebrate the publication of The Golden Legend (menu and seating plan in MCL).
The guests, in addition to Morris and Ellis, were Dr.
F.
J.
Furnivall, Edward Gordon Duff, Joseph Knight, Dr.
Richard Garnett, William Blaikie, William Michael Rossetti, Emery Walker, and C.
F.
Ellis.
However, the publication of the book was marred by the first of several misunderstandings between Morris and Quaritch.
In a letter to Walter T.
Spencer, 14 November 1894, Cockerell later summarized the episode: ‘The facts of the case are as follows: the Golden Legend was published not by us, but by Mr.
Quaritch, who on the day of publication raised the prices from £s.5.- to £6.6, and then by degrees to £1o.1o.-.
The trade, being dissatisfied with the terms which Mr.
Q. offered to supply them, did not push the book at the time, & the result was that a good many copies remained on Mr.
Q's hands.
These he is now attempting to sell at the original price £s.5.- to the public: with a discount of 15% to the trade'(LB).
Ellis prepared a modern-spelling, seven-volume edition of The Golden Legend for J.
M.
Dent's ‘Temple Classics’' in 19oo.

## A8
Raoul Lefevre, The Recuyell of the Historyes of Troye (2 vols.)

## [Vol
. i only] THE RECUYELL OF THE HISTORYES / OF TROYE.
ENGRAVED TITLE: the / Recuyell / of the / Historyes / of / Troye [gothic lettering designed by Morris].
COLOPHON: HERE ends this new edition of William Caxton's / Recuyell of the Historyes of Troy, done after the / First Edition; corrected for the press by H.
Halliday / Sparling, and printed by me William Morris at the / Kelmscott Press, Upper Mall, Hammersmith, in the / County of Middlesex, & finished on the fourteenth / day of October, 1892. / [paragraph] Sold by Bernard Quaritch, 15, Piccadilly. / [printer's mark 1] COLLATION: Large 4°: [Vol. i] a-t⁸ u4.
156 leaves, pp. [i-ij] iij-xv [xvj] [1] 2-295 [296]. [Vol. ii] x-2k8 21² (2l2+X1) m-3a8 3b² (3b²+2X1): 214 leaves, pp. [297] 298-507 [s08] [2] [509] 510-718 [4] [=428].

[Vol. ii] xı–2l₂r text of Book 112l2v-χı blank 2mı-3a6 text of Book 111 $3 ^ { \mathrm { a } } 7 ^ { \mathrm { r } }$ poem $3 { } ^ { 2 7 } { } ^ { \mathrm { v } }$ blank $3 \mathrm { a } 8 ^ { \mathrm { r } } { - } 3 \mathrm { b r } ^ { \mathrm { r } }$ glossary 3bı colophon $2 \chi \mathrm { I } ^ { \Gamma - 2 } \lambda ^ { \mathrm { v } }$ blank.
BINDING: Limp vellum with silk ties.
Stamped in gold on spine: 'TROYE / I [I·II]’ [enlarged Golden type].
PUBLICATION: 300 paper copies (9 gns.); 5 vellum copies $( \mathcal { L } 8 0 )$ .
Issued 24 November 1892.
NOTEs: In the summer of 189o Morris was trying todecide which Caxton title to reprint in order to launch his new Press. . . . now I have borrowed a Recueil of the Histories of Troy (the Wynkyn de Worde of course) from Quaritch,’ he wrote to Èllis on 29 August, and have no doubt that the L.L. [Golden Legend] is by far the most important book of the two' (Mackail, ii, 262).
Nevertheless, he eventually began printing the Recuyell – the first κP book in the Troy type and also the first in which the Chaucer type was employed (for prelims and end matter) – in February 1892.
In a memorandum to Quaritch on 1 June 1892, Morris outlined his plans for the book: ‘Reprinted from the first edition of Caxton's Translation – will make a quarto about 7oo p.p. which will if possible be bound in one vol: the whole edition will be delivered to the publisher bound in vellum or marocco [sic] to my satisfaction, so as to harmonize with the typography.
The book will be ornamented very richly with woodcut ornaments entirely designed by myself; no labour or expense will be spared on them and there will be much variety in them.
The semi-Ġothic type designed by me, (with special regard to legibility) will appear in this book for the first time'(BQ).
For Morris's contract with Quaritch, see E2.
Morris supplied the following note on the book for Quaritch's catalogue: As to the matter of the the book, it makes a thoroughly amusing story, instinct with mėdiæval thought and manners.
For though written at the end of the Middle Ages and dealing with classical mythology, it has in it no token of the coming “Renaissance" but is purely mediæval.
It is the last issue of that story of Troy which had such a hold on men's imaginations; the story built up from a rumour of the Cyclic Poets, of the heroic City of Troy, defended by Priam, with his gallant sons, led by Hector the preux Chevalier, beset by the violent & brutal Greeks, who were looked on as the necessary machinery for bringing about the undeniable tragedy of the fall of the city.
Surely this is well worth reading, if only as a piece of undiluted Mediævalism’(see a below).
There are several references to the book's progress in Cockerell's diary.
21 April 1892: ‘Took a sheet of the blackletter Recuyell to send to [John Ruskin at] Brantwood.'2 June 1892: Morris had several new side borders for the Recuyell etc.’28 July 1892: Morris had pages of Recuyell experimentally illuminated by [Edmond] Reuter.'6 September 1892: ‘Saw . . . pages of the Recuyell (68o now printed).’ The block for the engraved title was cut by C.
E.
Keates (Keates's bill, 3 October 1892: HEH HM 36661).
Altogether Quaritch paid Morris £1o12 1os. for the printing of the volume (receipts of 25 November 1892 and 28 February 1893 [BQ]).
The Star (London), 21 November 1892, p.
2, offered a vivid account of a pre-publication celebration at Kelmscott House: Mr.
William Morris . . . showed to a few friends on Tuesday last an advance copy of his reprint of Caxton's “Recueil of the Historyes of Troye." On looking through it, Dr.
Furnivall said enthusiastically, “It's the most beautiful book I ever saw; it's the most beautiful book ever printed!" Mr.
F.
S.
Ellis . . . cordially agreed in this opinion, and so did Mr.
Emery Walker . . . and Mr.
Theodore Watts. . . .
Though there had been a number of earlier editions of the Recuyell, the KP version was the first to go back directly to Caxton's text.
Nevertheless, H.
Oskar Sommer, in an edition of the Recuyell published by David Nutt in 1894, made a strong attack upon the textual reliability of the KP edition: In our own days . . .
Mr.
William Morris has produced . . . an edition of“The Recuyell," which, as regards getting up and artistic execution, is all that can be desired; but from my point of view, that of the critic and philologist, it is absolutely without any value.
I have often wondered how it is that Mr.
Morris, who is so rich in resources and designs, cannot find the means for the production of a beautiful book which at the same time meets all the requirements of modern scholarship.
If Mr.
Morris was determined to make the Trojan War the subject of one of his beautiful books, why did he not select Lydgate's Troy-Book, so infinitely superior to “The Recuyell," which from a purely literary point of view, has no value whatever?' Of the Recuyell, Reynard the Foxe (Aro), and Godefrey of Boloyne (Aı5), Sparling says: . . .
Morris wished them to be regarded as Kelmscott Press editions, and therefore to be amended where this was desirable.
Caxton's text was to be taken as a basis, but not looked upon as archaeologically sacrosanct.
It was to be collated with Caxton's originals, and corrected where need was, mistranslations being put right and omissions filled in, care being taken to preserve the style and flavour of Caxton in doing this'(p.
84).

Mackail (who was Burne-Jones's son-in-law and the first biographer of Morris) also produced two sequels for Longmans: The Sayings of the Lord Jesus, as Recorded by His Four Evangelists (1894) and Biblia Innocentium: Part Second.
Being the Story of God's Chosen People after the Coming of Our Lord Jesus Christ upon Earth, Written Anew for Children (19o1).
The Kelmscott edition was to have been illustrated with approximately two hundred wood-engravings designed by Burne-Jones, of which twenty-five designs were completed.
These – as redrawn by Robert Catterson-Smith - were published in The Beginning of the World: Twenty-Five Pictures by Edward Burne-Jones (1902).

In 1894 David Nutt published a metrical version, based on Caxton's text, by Ellis with illustrations by Walter Crane.
The book was announced as in the press in the Kp list of July 1892.
Printing actually began in late August or early September 1892.
Two proofs of the engraved title – which was cut by Keates (Keates's bill, 3 January 1893: HEH HM 36661) – are printed in red ink, possibly as an experiment (see b below).

## A1I
The Poems of William Shakespeare THE POEMS OF WILLIAM SHAKESPEARE, / PRINTED AFTER THE ORIGINAL / COPIES OF / VENUS AND ADONIS, 1593. / THE RAPE OF LUCRECE, 1594. / SON-NETS, 1609. / THE LOVER'S COMPLAINT.
HALF-TITLE ([a]4): VENUS AND ADONIS.
HALF-TITLE (e3): THE RAPE OF LUCRECE.
HALF-TITLE (i7r): SHAKESPEARE'S SONNETS.
COLOPHON: HERE ends this edition of Shakespeare's Poems, / edited by Frederick S.
Ellis, and printed by me / William Morris at the Kelmscott Press, Upper / Mall, Hammersmith, in the County of Middlesex, / and finished on the 17th day of January, 1893. / [printer's mark 1] / Sold by Reeves & Turner, 196, Strand.
FOREWORD: AFTER a lapse of about three hun-/dred years Shakespeare's Poems are / in this edition printed with the origi-/nal spelling.
That the orthography / of an author of the 16th or 17th cen-/tury cannot without detriment be / translated into that of the 19th, seems a proposition / too evident to leave room for controversy.
The fact / of being able to study these poems in the language / in which they were written, ought we think, to add / materially to the pleasure of the reader.

The sheets were sent to the binder on 2o January 1893, and on 14 February Leighton dispatched 25o copies to Reeves & Turner (Morris diary).
A rejected version of the title read: ‘The Poems & Sonnets / The Poems of William Shakespeare / Venus & Adonis / The Rape of Lucrece / The Sonnets / The Lovers Complaint'(see b below).
After the Poems was published, Ellis reported that F.
J.
Furnivall has been taking me roundly to task for some crimes committed in the Shakespeare's Poems – so much so that I am thinking of imprinting it yet once again' (Ellis to Francis J.
H.
Jenkinson, 9 August 1893 [CUL Add.
6463, no.
2416]).
Cockerell in 1898 described the volume as one of the rarest books issued from the Press' because of its popularity (List’).

Sold by Reeves & Turner, 196, Strand, / London. / [printer's mark 1] COLLATION: 80: [a]4 b-u⁸ x4.

M.
Gere, with caption in Golden type: [ornament 1] THIS IS THE PICTURE OF THE OLD / HOUSE BY THE THAMES TO WHICH / THE PEOPLE OF THIS STORY WENT [ornament 3] / HEREAFTER FOLLOWS THE BOOK IT-/SELF WHICH IS CALLED NEWS FROM / NO-WHERE OR AN EPOCH OF REST &/ IS WRITTEN BY WILLIAM MORRIS [two ornaments 3]'. coNTENTs: [a]1r-[a]1ν blank [a]2r title [a]2ν blank [a]3r-[a]3 table of contents [a]4r blank [a]4 frontispiece br-xır text x1 colophon x2r– x4 blank.
BINDıNG: Limp vellum with silk ties.
Stamped in gold on spine: ‘NEWS /FRÓM/NOWHERE /BY/WILLIAM/MORRIS' [Golden type].
PUBLICATION: 300 paper copies (2 gns.); 10 vellum copies (10 gns.).
Issued 24 March 1893.
NOTEs: New from Nowhere was printed in Commonweal, I January-4 October 189o.
The first book edition was published by Roberts Brothers of Boston in 189o, and the first English edition was issued by Reeves & Turner in March 1891.
The KP version‘was set up from a copy of one of Reeves & Turner's editions, and in reading it for the press the author made a few slight corrections'(List’).
The book was one of the earliest planned by Morris for his Press (Jane Morris to Wilfrid Scawen Blunt, 4 December [189o] [FM(1)]).
On 21 June 1892 Morris wrote to his daughter Jenny: We are printing off News from Nowhere at present, as the Compositors were overrunning the press men.
It looks very well'(BL Add.
Ms.
4534o, f.
85).
The volume was delayed, however, by its frontispiece of Kelmscott Manor.
The genesis and progress of Gere's frontispiece can be traced in three entries in Cockerell's diary.
17 October 1892: I suggested that a picture of the house should be given as frontispiece to News from Nowhere and w.M. seemed to like the idea.'1o November 1892: ‘Saw proofs of some drawings of Kelmscott made by Gere, who is to make an illustration for News from Nowhere.'24 January 1893: w.M. went to Kelmscott to meet Gere, who is drawing the house for “News from Nowhere".
Printing of the frontispiece – which was engraved by Hooper (HE H HM 3665s) – was completed on 7 March 1893 (Morris diary).
News from Nowhere was the last κP book, except for Epistola de Contemptu Mundi (A3ı), to use paragraph symbols.

Berger [1973]).

## A13
[Ramón Lull], The Order of Chivalry, and L'Ordene de Chevalerie with a translation by Morris THE ORDER OF CHIVALRY.
ENGRAVED TITLE: THE / ORDER OF / CHIVALRY [gothic lettering designed by Morris] CoLoPHoN (h3v): The Order of Chivalry, translated from / the French by William Caxton, edited by / F.
S.
Ellis, & printed by me William Morris / at the Kelmscott Press, Upper Mall, Ham-/mersmith, in the County of Middlesex, & / finished on the ioth day of November, 1892 / [paragraph] Sold by Reeves & Turner, 196, Strand, / London. / [printer's mark 1] HALF-TITLE (i): L'ORDENE DE CHEVALERIE, WITH / TRANSLATION BY WILLIAM MORRIS

## HALF-TITLE
(k4): THE ORDINATION OF KNIGHTHOOD.
COLOPHON (18r): THIS Ordination of Knighthood was / printed by William Morris at the Kelms-/cott Press, Upper Mall, Hammersmith, / in the County of Middlesex; finished on / the 24th day of February, 1893. / [printer's mark 1] EXTRACT FROM ELLIS'S MEMORANDA': [16V] THE “Order of Chivalry" was translated by Caxton from the French original, of which there are many early manuscripts in existence. . . . [paragraph] A strange confusion has been made by various writers & bibliographers between this treatise and a charming little French poem of the 13th century entitled “L’Ordene de Chevalerie." This was first printed at Paris in 1759, by M.
Barbazan, and again in the “Fabliaux et Contes," Paris, 1808, from the text found in the MS. volume 25462, fo.
149–157, in the National Library at Paris. . . . [l7r] The poem is here reprinted from the text given in the “Fabliaux et Contes,"1808. . . .
As the poem is ascribed to the 13th century, and the learned Director of the French National Library attributes the prose work to the 14th century, it might very well be that the author of the “Ordre de Chevalerie" was acquainted with the earlier work & might have been in some measure inspired by it. . . . [18r] The interest that it [The Order of Chivalry'] has now as an historical document is considerable, and the wonder is that it has not been reprinted before this time in our own days.
COLLTION: Smal1 40: [a]² b-g⁸ h4.
80: i–18.
78 leaves, pP. [4][1] 2-150 [151-2].
PAPER & DIMENSIONs: Flower(1) ([a]-h) and Flower(2) (i-l) paper.
Leaf: 200 × 138 mm.
Text: 28 lines × 88 mm.
TYPE: Chaucer type.
ORNAMENTS & ILLUSTRATION: Borders 9a and 4; corner borders; three-, six-, and ten-line initials; chapter titles in ‘The Order of Chivalry' in red; wood-engraved frontispiece designed by Burne-Jones.
CONTENTs: [a]ı title [a]1-[a]2r list of chapters [a4]2 frontispiece and engraved title br-h3 text of The Order of Chivalry h3 colophon h4r-h4 blank iır half-title iı blank i2r–k3r text of L'Ordene de Chevalerie l6v–18r ‘MEMORANDA CONCERNING THE / TWO PIECES HERE REPRINTED.'(by Ellis) l8r ¢olophon 18v blank.
BINDING: Limp vellum with silk ties.
Stamped in gold on spine: ‘THE ORDER OF CHIVALRY' [Troy type].
PUBLICATION: 225 paper copies (3os.); 10 vellum copies (10 gns.) Issued 12 April 1893.
NOTEs: There are frequent allusions to The Order of Chivalry in Cockerell's diary.
12 November 1892: ‘Saw the sheets of Caxton's Order of Chivalry the printing of which is just complete.
It is the prettiest book yet done.'3 December 1892: ‘w.M. began the translation of a French poem from which Caxton seems to have derived the idea of the Order of Chivalry.
It was wonderful to watch the words slipping into their places.' 27 January 1893: w.M. translating L’ordene de Chivalerie.'31 January 1893: w.M. experimenting with lettering for the Order of Chivalry cut.'13 February 1893: w.M. doing a border for the “Order of Chivalry"illustration.’2 March 1893: Morris showed Cockerell proofs of the illustrations for the Order of Chivalry'.
The inclusion of L'Ordene was an afterthought; hence the book's peculiar format.
(The first part of the volume represents the final use of the smaller Flower paper.) L'Ordene was initially translated into prose by Ellis before Morris decided to render it in verse (Mackail, ii, 295).
It was the first book printed in Chaucer type.
The reprint . . . was finished while News from Nowhere [A12] was in the press, and before Shakespeare's Poems and Sonnets [A11] was begun'(List’).
On 14 February 1893 Morris delivered the border to Hooper (Morris diary), who also engraved the frontispiece (Hooper's bill, 27 February 1893: HEH HM 36654).
Some of the three-line initials were designed for The Well at the World's End (A39) but were used here for the first time.
For a more recent scholarly edition, see The Book of the Ordre of Chyvalry, ed.
Alfred T.
P.
Byles (Early English Text Society, 1926).

## A14
George Cavendish, The Life of Cardinal Wolseγ THE LIFE OF THOMAS WOLSEY, / CARDINAL ARCH-BISHOP OF YORK / [ornament 1] WRITTEN BY GEORGE CAVEN-/DISH.
COLOPHON: Transcribed after the autograph manuscript of the / author, now in the British Museum, by F.
S.
Ellis, / and finished on the 2sth day of December, in the year / 1892, in the Parish of Cockington in the County / of Devon.
And printed by me William Morris, / at the Kelmscott Press, Upper Mall, Hammer-/smith in the County of Middlesex, and finished / on the 3oth day of March, 1893. / [printer's mark 1] / Sold by Reeves & Turner, 196, Strand.
FOREwORD: THE author of this life of Cardinal / Wolsey held the Office of gentil-/man usher in his household from / 1526 till 153o.
The book was writ-/ten about 1557.
It was not printed / till nearly a hundred years later, but / was well known through the many manuscript / copies of it which were handed about, all of which / however were more or less garbled & incomplete. / From one of these imperfect copies an edition was / printed in 164ı, & several other editions, all more / or less incomplete followed from time to time.
At / the beginning of the present century Mrs.
S.
W. / Singer had the good fortune to light upon the au-/tograph MS. of the author, from which he printed / an edition prefaced by an elaborate introduction / and supplemented by very useful notes and ap-/pendices.
He thought well however to modernize / the author's spelling, and occasionally to endeav-/our to clear or improve the style by slight altera-/tions.
In a few instances the author's words were / inadvertently changed.
The book is therefore now / for the first time put before the reader in the exact / form in which it was left by the author.
One spe-/cial point of interest about it is, that it presents us / with the first separate biography in the English / language.
But for it, we should have but a very imperfect idea of one of the most remarkable / figures in English history.
Chroniclers, historians, / and dramatists have alike been indebted to it in / writing of Wolsey.

NOTEs: The Ms which Singer used as the basis of his edition (2 vols., 1825) is BL Egerton 2402, which was acquired by the British Museum in 1876.
As the colophon indicates, Ellis used the Ms rather than Singer's edition, but the editor of a modern scholarly edition (Richard S.
Sylvester, ed., The Life and Death of Cardinal Wolsey by George Cavendish [Early English Text Society, 1959]) describes the KP version as ‘shot through with errors'(p. xi).
There are no references to the book in Cockerell's diary.
Morris, however, notes in his diary on 27 January 1893: first sheet of Wolsey to hand’.
One of the slip proofs (see a below) is initialled ‘H.H.'– probably H.
Howes, one of Morris's printers.

HISTORY OF GODEFREY OF BO-/LOYNE AND OF THE CONQUEST OF / IHERUSALEM.

Halliday Sparling, / and printed by me, William Morris, at the Kelms-/cott Press, Upper Mall, Hammersmith, in the / County of Middlesex, & finished on the 27th day / of April, 1893. / [printer's mark 2] / Sold by William Morris, at the Kelmscott Press.
COLLATION: Large 40: π8 [a]4 b−2f8 2g2.
238 leaves, pp. [i-ij] iij-xxij [2] [=24] [1] 2-450 [451-2].
PAPER & DIMENSIONS: Flower(2) paper.

Text: 32-3 lines × 126 mm.
TYPE: Text in Troy type; table of contents and glossary in Chaucer type.
ORNAMENTs: Borders 5a and 6; side, corner, half, and three-quarter borders; six- and eight-line initials; chapter titles in red.
CONTENTs: π title πI blank π2-π5 foreword (by Caxton) π5ν- [a]3 table of contents [a]4 blank [a]4 engraved title brr-2gı text 2gi Caxton's colophon 2g2 glossary 2g2 colophon.
BINDING: Limp vellum with silk ties.
Stamped in gold on spine: GODEFREY/ OF / BOLOYNE' [enlarged Troy type].
PUBLICATION: 300 paper copies (6 gns.); 6 vellum copies (2o gns.).
Issued 24 May 1893.
NOTEs: The following note in the announcement (C5) of Godefrey was written by Morris: ‘This book must from a literary point of view be considered one of Caxton's most important works.
It is not a “romance," as it has been called by Dibdin, Blades, and others, but a very serious piece of history, the original of which is William of Tyre's Historia Rerum in partibus transmarinis Gestarum; from the French translation of which Caxton made his English version, bringing it to an end just after the death of Godfrey, whereas the Archbishop of Tyre carries on the history for many years after.
The style of Caxton's book is vigorous and agreeable, and often reads like the most dramatic of all historic works, Lord Berner's translation of Froissart, and altogether it is a delightful book to read.
8 The engraved title (reduced) of The History of Godefrey of Boloyne (Aı5), designed by Morris.
There are many new ornaments in this the latest issue from the Kelmscott Press; for the chapters being very short and the scheme of ornament being very similar to that of the last two quartos, the Reynard and the Recuyell, the book is very decorative.
The book does indeed contain a profusion of ornaments, many of which were engraved by Keates (see h below).
The larger printer's mark was also used for the first time.
In the proofs (see f below) there are numerous notes from Morris to his printers about the ornaments: e.g., ‘½ border 1o [–] if it will not come in ½ border 9. but get it in if you can'(p.
341) and ½ border 5 - but take care it does not print heavy'(p.
243).
Quaritch was very annoyed by Morris's decision to publish this, the last of the five Caxton reprints, himself: Mr.
W.
Morris wishes to have all the profits of the Kelmscott press; he will retail himself the [’]Godfrey of Bouloyne" – he will be a loser by it, but it will be a nuisance to me on account of the Chaucer'(Quaritch to B.
Alfred Quaritch, 8 February 1893 [Bod.
Ms.
Eng. lett. c.
435, f.
189]).
Quaritch's prediction was accurate, for Godefrey did not sell well, and in 1897 Mrs.
Morris donated a number of copies to public libraries.
Morris showed the first copy of Godefrey to Cockerell and others on the evening of 11 May 1893 (Cockerell diary).

Issued 8 September 1893.
NOTEs: The suggestion to print More's Utopia apparently originated with Cockerell on 29 February 1892 (Cockerell diary).
The book was printed during July and early August 1893, and the cost of production was £255 14s.
9d.
(Ledger).
Morris wrote the foreword on 12 July 1893 (Morris to Jenny Morris, 13 July 1893 [BL Add.
Ms.
45340, f.
122]).
The first English edition of Utopia was published in 1551 and the second edition (which Morris owned [Library, no. $6 4 7 ] ) \textrm { - }$ newlie perused and corrected, and also with divers notes in the margent augmented' – in 1556.
The printer of both editions was Abraham Vele.
In a presentation copy of the KP volume, Ellis wrote: I cannot help feeling vexed at my name appearing as Editor opposite a palpable misprint $[ \mathrm { } ^ { \bullet \bullet } \mathrm { \overline { { W } } e a l e } ^ { \prime \bullet } ]$ of the first printer's name.
I did correct it, but my proof was lost and the misprint left' (see o below).
Robinson's translation had been reprinted several times during the nineteenth century.
Morris at one time intended to reproduce a map of Utopia that appeared in the first Latin edition of the work (see c below).
Aymer Vallance (William Morris [1897], p.
40o) records an amusing anecdote: ‘Of the 3oo copies issued, 4o had been ordered in advance by an Eton master, with the intention of distributing them as prizes among the boys of the college, but when the work appeared with a compromisingly Socialistic introduction by Morris, the order, from motives of pruderce, had to be cancelled.
However the copies were all disposed of before a year was out, so Morris did not suffer any loss.' After the publication of the κP edition of Maud, Morris confessed to Blunt: I would sooner . . . have printed them [Macmillan & Co.] Tennyson's first volume, which is all I ever cared for in his poems'(Blunt diary, 6 October 1893).
On a later occasion Morris told Blunt that Tennyson ‘never forgave him and Burne-Jones for having disapproved of his bowdlerization of the Morte d’Arthur in the “Idylls of the $\mathrm { K i n g } ^ { \prime \prime }$ (Blunt diary, 29 May 1896).

(Ledger), and for the 5oo paper copies delivered to Macmillan he charged £,4oo (see E5).
'The borders were specially designed for this book.
They were both used again in the Keats [A24], and one of them appears in The Sundering Flood $\left[ \mathrm { A } \varsigma \mathrm { I } \right] ^ { 3 } \left( ^ { \circ } \mathrm { L i s t } ^ { 3 } \right)$ On 14 November 1893 Bertram Dobell, the bookseller, wrote to Macmillan that a customer had returned his copy of Maud complaining of typographical errors on pp.
19, 26, and 69.
Two days later Morris replied to Macmillan: I am sorry to say that as to the places quoted in Mr.
Dobell's note, the facts are as stated; but having been very carefully over the book I can see no other errors.
I will go over it again and when I have found out what the worst is, we will consider what is best to be done'(HEH HM 36673).
As a result, four cancellans leaves were printed $\left( \mathrm { p p . ~ I 5 - I 6 , ~ I 9 - 2 0 , ~ } 2 .
5 \mathrm { - } 6 , 6 \mathrm { - } 7 0 \right)$ and were perhaps inserted in at least a few copies of Maud.

26, 1.
18, ‘where’should be ‘were'; p.

## A19
William Meinhold, Sidonia the Sorceress SIDONIA THE SORCERESS [ornament 1] BY WILLIAM / MEINHOLD TRANSLATED BY FRANCESCA / SPE-RANZA LADY WILDE.
COLoPHoN: Here ends the story of Sidonia the Sorceress, translated from the / German of William Meinhold, by Francesca Speranza, Lady / Wilde, and now reprinted by me, William Morris, at the Kelms-/cott Press, Upper Mall, Hammersmith, in the County of Middle-/sex.
Finished on the 15th day of September, 1893. / [printer's mark 2] / Sold by William Morris, at the Kelmscott Press.
COLLATION: Large 40: [a]8 b−2f8 2g4.
236 leaves, pp. [2] [i-ii] iii-xiv [=16] [1] 2-455 [456].
PAPER & DIMENSIONs: Flower(2) paper.
Leaf: 287×205 mm.
Text: 41 lines × 126 mm.
TYPE: Golden type.
ORNAMENTS: Border 8; six- and ten-line initials; chapter titles, speakers’ names, and side notes in red.
CONTENTs: [a]-[a]1ν blank [a]2r title [a]2ν blank [a]3r–[a]5r preface (by Meinhold) [a]5 epistle from Theodore Plonnies to Bogislaff the Fourteenth, the last Duke of Pomerania [a]6–[a]8 table of contents bīr-2g4 text 2g4 colophon.
BINDING: Limp vellum with silk ties.
Stamped in gold on spine: ‘SI-DONIA / THE / SORCERESS' [Troy tγpe].
PUBLICATION: 300 paper copies (4 gns.); 10 vellum copies (20 gns.) Issued 1 November 1893.
NOTES: Morris wrote the following note for the announcement (C9) of Sidonia: SIDONIA THE SORCERESS, the translation of which is now on the point of being issued by the Kelmscott Press, is an Historical Romance, based more or less on fact, concerning the Witch Fever that afflicted Northern Europe during the latter half of the ith & first half of the 16th centuries.
It was written by William Meinhold, a Lutheran minister, dwelling in the island of Rugen, off the shore of Pomerania, a man so steeped in the history and social life of his country during the period above-mentioned, that he might almost be said to have been living in it, rather than in his own, the early part of the present century.
The result of his life and literary genius was the production of two books: “The Amber Witch," & “Sidonia the Sorceress," both of which, but, in my judgment, especially “Sidonia," are almost faultless reproductions of the life of the past; not mere antiquarian studies, but presentations of events, often tragic, the actors in which are really alive, though under conditions so different from those of the present day.
In short, “Sidonia"is a masterpiece of its kind, and without a rival of its kind.
It must be added that it was a great favourite with the more literary part of the pre-Raphaelite artists in the earlier days of that movement.
The two beautiful water-colour pictures of Sidonia and of Clara von Dewitz, by Mr.
Burne-Jones, which the general public had the opportunity of seeing at the exhibition of his pictures this spring, caused many questions to be asked about this, which the present edition of the book will answer satisfactorily.
Lady Wilde's translation, which was the one through which we made acquaintance with Meinhold's genius, is a good, simple, and sympathetic one.' Lady Wilde's translation of Sidonia the Sorceress, which was published in the Parlour Library in 1849, was well known to Morris's circle: Rossetti in particular was an enthusiastic admirer of it, and Burne-Jones produced two water-colours based on the story in 186o.
Oscar Wilde's mother doubtless appreciated Morris's cheque for £25 (see d below), for she was living in extreme poverty in the 189os.
Through the intervention of Aymer Vallance, Aubrey Beardsley prepared a drawing of Sidonia intended as a frontispiece, which he showed to Morris in the spring or early summer of 1892.
Morris's comments, though he praised Beardsley's feeling for draperies', were so discouraging that the young artist abandoned the attempt, and thereafter Beardsley expressed great hostility towards Morris and the κP (Vallance, ‘The Invention of Aubrey Beardsley', Magazine of Art, May 1898, pp.
363-4; Vallance, ‘List of Drawings by Aubrey Beardsley', in Robert Ross, Aubrey Beardsley [1921], p.
71).
Work began on Sidonia in the first half of 1893 (Ledger); on 13 April 1893 Morris recorded in his diary: I have now got 2½ sections of Sidonia printed.' Cost of production was £s84 18s.
(Ledger).
The book did not sell briskly: on 1o October 1894 Cockerell instructed Leighton to bind thirty copies ‘in half holland uniform with the Golden Legend'(LB), and these were later donated to British and American libraries.
After Sidonia's publication, F.
S.
Ellis wrote to Morris on 6 January 1894 listing the printer's errors he had found in it: I have just finished reading aloud “Sidonia"& am more impressed than ever with its excellence. . . .
To come to the point there are certainly more misprints than there ought to be.
Pray dont think that I consider myself perfect as a press-reader, far from it & it is a consciousness of the difficulty of one man getting a thing perfect that makes me propose that you should send me a proof simultaneously with your other reader, simply on the principle that four eyes are better than two.
In case you have not had your attention called to what I mean I will be the “damned good natured friend"[.] p.
195 (1.
1), “rings" for “wrings” p.
35o note 1.
3 “Heredotus” p.
355 Šide note.

8.
“Silvertre" for “Silvestre” p.
415.
1.
27 “navitate" for “nativitate” p.
419.
1.
15 “coach" for “couch” p.
437.
1.
9 “hert" for “her" p.
441.
1.
28.
“morning” for “mourning”.
These are but a few that I have noted, for when I had no penci at hand I didn't mark ’em.
The same (in a less degree) is the case with “Godfrey".
Perhaps I am one of the few people who have read all the books you have printed' (BL Add.
Ms.
52715, f.
11).

## Azo
Dante Gabriel Rossetti, Ballads and Narrative Poems BALLADS AND NARRATIVE POEMS / BY DANTE GA-BRIEL ROSSETTI.
ENGRAVED TITLE: BALLADS / AND /NARRATIVE / POEMS BY / DANTE / GABRIEL / ROSSETTI [roman lettering designed by Morris] CoLoPHoN: [ornament 1] Here ends the Book of Ballads and Narrative / Poems, written by Dante Gabriel Rossetti, and / printed by William Morris at the Kelmscott / Press, 14 Upper Mall, Hammersmith, in the / County of Middlesex; finished on the 14th day / of October, of the year 1893. / [printer's mark 1] / Published by Ellis & Elvey, 29 New Bond Street.
COLLATION: 80: [a]4 b-p8 q4.
120 leaves, PP. [8] [1] 2-227 [228] [4] [=232].
PAPER & DIMENSIONS: Flower(2) paper.
Leaf: 205 × I40 mm.
Text: 27–31 lines × 92 mm.
TYPE: Golden type.
ORNAMENTs: Borders 4a and 4; six- and ten-line initials; some lines and shoulder titles in red.
CONTENTS: $\ [ \mathsf { a } ] _ { } \mathbf { I } ^ { \mathsf { r } } - \bigl [ \mathsf { a } \bigr ] \mathsf { 2 ^ { v } }$ blank [a]3r title [a]3 table of contents [a]4 blank [a]4 engraved title bīr–q1 text q2 colophon q2-q4 blank.
BinDiNG: Limp vellum with silk ties.
Stamped in gold on spine: ‘BALLADS/AND /POEMS/BY/D.G./ROSSETTI'[reduced Golden type].
PUBLICATION: 310 paper copies (2 gns.); 6 vellum copies (10 gns.).
Issued November 1893.
NOTES: In the Ellis papers at UCLA there are frequent references to this and its companion volume, Sonnets and Lyrical Poems by Dante Gabriel Rossetti (A2oa).
Early in July 1893 Morris agreed to print Rossetti's poems for Ellis & Elvey, recommending that they be divided into two volumes and that his charge per copy (for each volume) would be 2ıs.
(see E6).
By 12 July Ellis & Elvey reported to William Michael Rossetti, who held his brother's literary rights, that the first volume – which Morris suggested calling Ballads and Tales in Verse – would be ready by the end of November.
The title was slightly altered at the request of W.
M.
Rossetti, who read proofs and carefully supervised the book.
One issue that arose during the printing of Sonnets and Lyrical Poems was whether the sonnet Nuptial Sleep'should be restored to The House of Life.
D.
G.
Rossetti had deleted the sonnet in 1872 after it was brutally attacked by Robert Buchanan in his notorious essay ‘The Fleshly School of Poetry', and William Michael, in a preface to his brother's Collected Works (1886), explained that ‘his own decision commands mine'(i, xxxiii).
W.
M.
Rossetti again declined to allow the sonnet to appear in the KP edition; he restored it to the text of The House of Life only in 1904.
W.
M.
Rossetti submitted the table of contents for the second volume in early November 1893, and on 27 December Ellis & Elvey sent him proofs of the first sheet.
The KP Ledger is not entirely clear in distributing costs between the two volumes, but it appears that the production cost of Sonnets and Lyrical Poems was £2o2 5s.
On 6 July 1894 Ellis & Elvey wrote to W.
M.
Rossetti: ‘The Kelmscott Press Editions have we think done fairly well [during the first half of the year].
264 copies of vol ı & 265 of vol ı1 have been sold altogether.' Meanwhile, in America, Copeland & Day had early in 1894 produced an edition of The House of Life, done in close imitation of the Kelmscott style, which troubled both Morris and Ellis & Elvey.
However much we may disapprove of this publication, & the bad imitation of the Kelmscott Press we fear we cannot help it,’Ellis & Elvey declared to Rossetti on 1 February 1894.
(Copeland & Day had sent a copy of the book to Ellis & Elvey.) RELATED MATERIALS: (a) Proof of p.
I (PML ‘Morrisiana II’).
(b) Pres. copy to Cockerell, 3 February 1894, with original designs by Morris for eight initials (five in an early state) and a letter from Sir E.
Burne-Jones to Sir Sydney Cockerell asking for a copy of the Poems' (Cockerell sale, no.
23).
(c) Pres. copy to Burne-Jones, 17 December 1893 (PM L).
(d) Pres. copy to Philip Webb, 14 December 1893 (τC).
(e) Pres. copy to Jenny Morris (Fm[1]).
(f) Harry Buxton Forman's copy (BC).
(g) Pres. copy to Kate Faulkner, 14 December 1893 (Parke-Bernet cat., 26 November 1975, no.
742).
(h) Pres. copy to Georgiana Burne-Jones, 29 December 1893 (Sotheby cat., 22 December 1952, no.
57).
(i) Copy (vellum) with Morris's bookplate and note by Cockerell: ‘Sold to Henry Gamman, August 1897'(PcL).
(j) Pres. copy to Robert W.
Smith, 23 January 1894 (Merwin-Clayton Sales Company [New York] cat., 4 March 1912, no.
374).

## THE
TALE OF KING FLORUS / AND THE FAIR JEHANE.

TYPE: Chaucer type.
ORNAMENTS: Borders Ia and II; three- and six-line initials; title on bı, explicit $( \mathbf { g } 8 ^ { \mathrm { v } } )$ , and shoulder notes in red.
CONTENTS: [a]ı pastedown $[ \mathsf { a } ] \mathsf { 2 ^ { r } } \mathsf { - a } \mathsf { 3 ^ { v } }$ blank $[ { \mathfrak { a } } ] _ { 4 } \mathfrak { r }$ title $[ \mathfrak { a } ] 4 ^ { \mathrm { v } _ { - } } \pi \mathbf { I } ^ { \mathrm { r } }$ blank $\pi \mathbf { I } ^ { \mathbf { V } }$ engraved title $\mathrm { \ b r - [ h ] \it { 4 } ^ { v } }$ text $\chi \mathbf { r }$ colophon $X ^ { \mathrm { { I } ^ { V } } }$ blank.

Text of title-page printed in black on front cover.
PUBLICATION: 35O paper copies $( 7 s . \ 6 d . )$ ; 15 vellum copies (3os.).
Issued 28 December 1893.
NOTES: The source of this and two other KP books – Of the Friendship of Amis and Amile (A23) and The Tale of the Emperor Coustans and of Over Sea (A26) – was Nouvelles françaises en prose du xiiième siècle by L.
Moland and C.
D'Hericault (Paris, 1856).
Morris's translations were later collected in a single volume entitled Old French Romances Done into English by William Morris, published by George Allen in 1896 with an introduction by Joseph Jacobs, who described the tales ‘as a sort of outhouse to that goodly fabric so appropriately known to us all as The Earthly Paradise'(p. xxv).
Allen, incidentally, wished to reprint the KP engraved titles, but Morris declined permission (Cockerell to Allen, 23 November 1895 [LB]).
Hooper's bills for engraving the titles of King Florus (24 November 1893) and Amis and Amile (5 March 1894) are in HEH HM 36655.
King Florus was read for the press by Ellis, who added this characteristic note to Morris in the proofs (see d below): ‘I think this may go for press.
There are singularly few typographical errors.
I daresay you will despise some of my queries & questions.
Cost of production was £71 16s.
1d.
(Ledger).
Seventy-six sets of sheets of King Florus were distributed by Tregaskis to the world's leading binders, and the results were exhibited in London between 27 June and 7 July 1894.
(See the Studio, 3 [July 1894], 125-6, and A Memorable Exhibit of Bookbinding', British Printer, 7 [July-August 1894], 13.) These copies are now in the John Rylands Library, Manchester.

## A22

WRITTEN BY / WILLIAM MORRIS.
ENGRAVED TITLE: the story / of the / Glittering / Plain / or the / Land of / Living / Men [gothic lettering designed by Morris] COLOPHON: HERE ends the tale of the Glittering Plain, written / by William Morris, & ornamented with 23 pictures / by Walter Crane.
Printed at the Kelmscott Press, / Upper Mall, Hammersmith, in the County of Mid-/dlesex, & finished on the 13th day of January, 1894. / [printer's mark 2] / Sold by William Morris, at the Kelmscott Press.

96 leaves, PP. [8] [1] 2-177 [178-80] [4] [=184].

TYPE: Text in Troy type; table of contents in Chaucer type.

(The accompanying borders and text are not reproduced.) ORNAMENTS & ILLUSTRATIONS: Borders I2a and 12; side and threequarter borders; three-, five-, six-, and eight-line initials; chapter titles in red; wood-engraved illustrations designed by Walter Crane on [a]4ν (p.
1), b2ν (p.
4), b3 (p.

10), b7 (p.
13), c1r (p.17), c7r $\begin{array} { r } { \left( \vec { \mathrm { p } } \cdot \mathsf { \bar { \Phi } } 2 9 \right) , \mathsf { d } _ { 3 } ^ { \mathrm { { r } } } \left( \mathsf { p } . \mathsf { \Phi } _ { 3 } \vec { \mathrm { 7 } } \right) , \mathrm { ~ e r v } \left( \mathsf { p } . \mathsf { \bar { \Phi } } \circ \right) , \mathrm { ~ e 4 ^ { \mathrm { r } } ~ } ( \mathsf { p } . \mathsf { \Phi } _ { 5 } \mathscr { S } ) , \mathrm { ~ f r ~ } ( \mathsf { p } . \mathsf { \Phi } _ { 6 } \mathscr { S } ) , \mathrm { ~ f 4 ^ { \mathrm { r } } ~ } ( \mathsf { p } . \mathsf { \Phi } _ { 7 } \mathrm { I } ) , \mathrm { ~ f 7 ^ { \mathrm { { r } } } ~ } \left( \mathsf { p } \cdot \mathsf { \bar { \Phi } } \right) , } \end{array}$ (p.

CONTENTS: $\big [ \mathrm { a } \big ] \mathrm { I } ^ { \mathrm { r } } { - } \big [ \mathrm { a } \big ] 2 ^ { \mathrm { v } }$ blank [a]3r title [a]3 table of contents [a]4r blank [a]4 engraved title br-n2r text n2 colophon $\scriptstyle { \mathrm { n } } 2 ^ { \mathrm { v } } - { \mathrm { n } } 4 ^ { \mathrm { v } }$ blank.
BINDING: Limp vellum with silk ties.
Stamped in gold on spine: ‘THE GLITTÈRING PLAIN' [Troy type].
PUBLICATION: 250 paper copies (s gns.); 7 vellum copies $( \mathcal { L } 2 0 )$ .
Issued 17 February 1894.
NOTEs: This has the odd distinction of being the only title printed twice at the KP.
Morris was so eager to get the first edition (Ai) into print that he would not wait for Crane's illustrations; but later (probably in the autumn of 1892) Morris again approached Crane about illustrating a new edition (see F3), with the profits from the book to be split between them.
Crane earned at least £Iso for his contribution to the book (Cockerell to A.
J.
R.
Gooderson, 27 September 1894 [L B]).
Crane's designs were cut on wood by A.
Leverett.
I think your woodcuts look delightful,' Morris wrote to Crane on 17 February 1894 (letter privately printed by William P.
Barlow, Jr., 1975).
Crane himself, however, doubted ‘if I was ever quite Gothic enough in feeling to suit his [Morris's] taste' (The Work of Walter Crane’, Art Journal, Easter Annual, 1898, p.
10).
Philip Webb, in a letter to Morris, 18 February 1894, commented: ‘The book is quite up to the Thames-side mark in all but the pictures, and these, if one is careful in looking, have their value: but Crane will not (or can-not) take pains.
A lot of work in these blocks is good – really good – but the inaptitudes are abundant. . . .
However, do not mind – the whole book looks delightful; and, for the pictures . . . why, they are more than helped out by the borders, and the heavyline engraving of the subjects.
But the features; why, they are killed by Crane's no-notion of surface, & of black[,] white and grey.
Also, costume is before the figure it clothes.
I cannot see the difficulty with a knowledge of old time – of inventing costume.
Alack – but still, it don't much matter; but, again: do not be so rash in trusting the picture makers in future.
How can they know?'(v & A[1] 86.TT.3).
Cockerell later added this note to Webb's letter: ‘Morris was no less dissatisfied than Webb with Crane's illustrations to his Glittering Plain & thought this volume his one Kelmscott Press failure.
Morris designed the borders in late 1892 and early 1893 (Cockerell diary, 1 November 1892; Morris diary, 18 March 1893). ‘Neither the borders in this book nor six out of the seven frames round the illustrations appear in any other book.
The seventh is used round the second picture in Love is Enough [A52]’(List’).
The engraved title was cut by Hooper (Hooper's bill, 15 December 1893: HEí M 36655).
The copytext was not the earlier KP edition but the edition printed in 1891 at the Chiswick Press (see c below).
The names of the following compositors appear in the marked text: H.
H.[owes], Binning, Tippett, and Collins.
The cost of production was £485 18s.
8d.
(Ledger).
The book sold slowly, and on i1 October 1894 Cockerell instructed Leighton to ‘bind up the 31 unbound Glittering Plains in half holland . . .' (LB).
RELATED MATERIALS: (a) Cockerell's copy, with MS of colophon, trial page, and Ms agreement in Morris's hand to prepare an edition of The Glittering Plain illustrated by Crane (Cockerell sale, no.

BINDING: Limp vellum with silk ties.
Stamped in gold on spine: ‘THE POEMŚ / OF / JOHN KEATS.' [Ġolden type].
PUBLICATION: 300 paper copies (3os.); 7 vellum copies (9 gns.).
Issued 8 May 1894.
NOTEs: On 25 May 1893 Ellis wrote to Harry Buxton Forman: Morris is printing at the Kelmscott Press a small edition of certain Poems of Keats – al those which he considers worth printing & I am seeing it through the press for him. ‘Would you mind telling me if any of Keats' Poems are at present copyright, as he would of course omit them.
It will simply [be] a handsomely printed volume without notes of any kind' (BC).
Sheet y had to be reprinted because the wrong version of Keats's La Belle Dame sans Merci’was used.
A slightly garbled text of the poem had appeared in Leigh Hunt's Indicator in 182o and was occasionally reprinted thereafter, and it was this inferior version which had been set up by the KP compositors.
Cockerell records Morris's reaction: In February 1894 the last sheets of the Kelmscott Press Keats . . . were being printed.
A specimen of each sheet of every book was brought in to Morris as soon as it came off the press.
I was with him when he happened to open the sheet on which La Belle Dame sans Merci was printed.
He began to read it and was suddenly aware of unfamiliar words. . . .
Great was his indignation.
He swiftly altered the words and then read the poem to me, remarking that it was the germ from which all the poetry of his group had sprung – The sheet was reprinted and the earlier and better version restored - I still have the cancelled sheet with his corrections'(Sidney Colvin, John Keats: His Life and Poetry, His Friends, Critics and After-Fame [1918], p.
470).
As an undergraduate Morris owned the 1854 edition of Keats’s Poetical Works (now at km), and his own poetry, as he admitted, shows indebtedness to Keats (see Clarice Short, William Morris and Keats’, PMLA, 59 [June, 1944], 513–23).
Cockerell saw the first page of the KP Keats on 8 October 1893 (diary).
Cost of production was £372 8s.
1od.
(Ledger).
The book was popular and went out of print very quickly.
RELATED MATERIALS: (a) Inc. corrected proofs, including rejected version of ‘La Belle Dame sans Merci’ (BL C.43.c.26 [ΣQ26]).

He read out the poem to me and added,"That was the beginning of all our school"'(Cockerell's note) (PMLMorrisiana 11’).

Fr.
Mel.
2o (s37). [Golden type] ENGRAVED TITLE: Atalanta / in Calydon / a tragedy / made by / Algernon / Charles / Swinburne [gothic lettering designed by Morris] COLOPHON: HERE ends Atalanta in Calydon, a Tragedy, made / by Algernon Charles Swinburne, and printed by / William Morris, at the Kelmscott Press, Upper / Mall, Hammersmith, in the County of Middlesex; / finished on the 4th day of May, 1894.
Note, that the / Greek letters in this book were designed by Selwyn / Image for Messrs.
Macmillan & Co., who have kindly / allowed them to be used here./ [printer's mark 2] / Sold by William Morris, at the Kelmscott Press.
COLLATION: Large 4º: [a]⁴ b-f⁸ g⁴.
46 leaves, pp. [8] [1] 2-81 [82-4].
PAPER & DIMENSIONS: Perch paper.
Leaf: 289 × 2I0 mm.
Text: 31 lines × 130 mm.
TYPE: Golden type.
ORNAMENTs: Borders 5a and 5; side and three-quarter borders; three-, six-, eight-, and eleven-line initials; row of ornaments on [a]2ν and speakers’ names (usually in margin) in red.
CoNTENTs: [a]ı title and Greek epigraph [a]ı-[a]2ν dedication to Walter Savage Landor and poem in Greek [a]3r dramatis personæ and quotation from AEschylus [a]3 argument [a]4 blank [a]4 engraved title bır–gı text gı colophon g2r-g4 blank.
BINDING: Limp vellum with silk ties.
Stamped in gold on spine: ‘ATALANTA.
SWINBURNE'[Golden type; second word reduced].
PUBLICATION: 250 paper copies (2 gns.); 8 vellum copies (12 gns.).
Issued 24 July 1894.
NOTEs: Swinburne was a warm admirer of the KP books, and he and Morris maintained a friendly but not intimate relationship.
Wilfrid Scawen Blunt recorded in his diary on 26 May 1896: ‘Swinburne's new poem was reviewed yesterday in all the papers.
Morris thinks it poor stuff and not worth doing as the story Balin and Balan was quite perfect in its prose form in the Morte d’Arthur.
“It will not do however" he said “for Swinburne to hear me saying this, for he would never forgive me".
Swinburne, it appears[,] is the most sensitive and jealous of men and cannot bear the smallest criticism, but he and Morris have not met for years though Mrs.
Morris goes now and then to see Swinburne.'When Atalanta (which was first published in 1865) was printed by Morris, Swinburne wrote to him that it was certainly one of the loveliest examples of even your incomparable press'(John Y.
LeBourgeois, ‘Some Unpublished letters of Swinburne', Notes and Queries, NS 19 [July 1972], 262).
Morris's own comment, in a letter to Theodore Watts, 9 August 1894, was ‘I like the look of the volume very much'(CNY).
This is the only κP book in which Morris used a type not designed by himself.
During 1892 Selwyn Image, with the technical assistance of Emery Walker, designed a Greek type based ‘on a certain tenth century Ms in the B.
Museum, and on a Spanish printed book there of the fifteenth or early sixteenth century, which was itself founded on a Ms of the 1oth century'(Seluyn Image Letters, ed.
A.
H.
Macmurdo [1932], p.
75).
A series of letters from Image to Macmillan & Co. about the designing of the type is in BL Add.
Ms.
5526o.
The type was first used in Archer Hind's edition of the Phædo (1894).
On 27 April 1894 Morris wrote to Macmillan: . . .
I venture to ask you to be kind enough to lend me some of your new Greek type for the quotations & dedicatory poems.
In the event of your not wishing the type to leave your hands, the passages might perhaps be set up by you & stereotyped – in which case I may mention that I propose to have them in caps throughout, without leading or indentation (BL Add.
Ms.
5526o, f.
176).
In the end, Macmillan supplied electrotypes.
In the vellum copies of Atalanta, the colophon appears on g2r.
Cost of production was £27o 1os.
6d.
(Ledger).
The book was sold out within à few weeks of publication.
RELATED MATERIALS: (a) Pres. copy to Cockerell, 14 August 1894, with design for engraved title, Ms in Morris's hand of colophon, designs for twelve initials, ‘a note by him of the copies of the book he proposed to give to his family and friends, and a letter to Sir Sydney Cockerell from Selwyn Image referring to the Greek type designed by him and used in this book'(Cockerell sale, no.
25; Sotheby cat., 22 May 1972, no.
148).

CHEWOODBEYONDTHEWORLD Chapter I.
Of Golden alter and hís father WHILE AGO τ∈R∈ 飄AS HYOANGMAN DELLINGIN HGREHTHND goodly city by the sea which had tonameLangton on Holm.
He was but of fve and twenty wínters, a faírsfaced man, yellowshaired,tall and strong; rather wiser than foolisher than young men are mostly wont; a valiant youth,& a kind; not of many words but courteous of speech;noroisterer,noughtmasterful, but peaceable and knowing how to fors bear: ín a fray a perílous foe, & a trusty warsfellow.
His father, with whom he was dwelling when this tale begins,was a great merchant, richer than a baron of the land, a headsman of the greatest of the Líneages of Langton, and a captaín of the Porte; he was of the Líneage of the Goldings, therefore was he called 12 The first text page (reduced) of The Wood beyond the World (A27).
The chapter title is in red. ‘The borders in this book, as well as the ten half-borders, are here used for the first time' (‘List’).
The quarter sheet d2.7 (pp.
35–6, 45–6) had to be reprinted because the phrase (on p.
46) ‘somewhat uneasy going' was mistakenly printed as‘somewhat easy going'(see e below).
Burne-Jones was paid f2o for designing the frontispiece; the total cost of production was £33o 18s.
5d.
(Ledger).
By the date of publication – which Cockerell gives in a letter (to Miss E.
M.
Evans, 19 October 1894 [LB]) as 18 rather than 16 October – all but a few copies had been sold.
On 21 February 1895 Morris arranged for a cheap edition to be issued by Lawrence & Bullen: his royalties from that edition in 1895 were £69 (Cockerell diary, 21 February 1895 and 20 January 1896).

## THE
BOOK OF WISDOM AND LIES.
ENGRAVED TITLE: THE BOOK / OF / WISDOM / AND LIES / [arms of Georgia: the Holy Coat] / ARMA GEORGIAE [roman lettering designed by Morris] COLOPHoN: HERE endeth The Book of Wisdom & Lies, a / Georgian Story-book of the eighteenth century, / by Sulkhan-Saba Orbeliani: translated, with / notes, by Oliver Wardrop.
Printed by William / Morris at the Kelmscott Press, 14 Upper Mall, / Hammersmith, in the County of Middlesex; / & finished on the 29th day of September, 1894. / [printer's mark 1] / Sold by Bernard Quaritch, 15 Piccadilly, W.
COLLATION: 80: [a]10 b-r8.
138 leaves, pp. [2] [i] ii-xvi [2] [=20] [ı] 2-256.
PAPER & DIMENSIONS: Flower(2) paper.
Leaf: 207 × 140 mm.
Text: 30 lines × 88 mm.
TYPE: Golden type.
ORNAMENTs: Borders 4a and 4; six-line initials; footnotes, chapter titles, and shoulder notes in red.
CoNTENTs: [a]ı-[a]ı blank [a]2 title [a]2v-[a]6 table of contents [a]6v-[a]9 introduction (by Wardrop) [a]1o blank [a]1o engraved title b1r-r8r text r8 colophon.
BINDING: Limp vellum with silk ties.
Stamped in gold on spine: THE BOOK/ OF / WISDOM / AND LIES.'[reduced Gρlden type].
PUBLICATION: 25o paper copies (2 gns.); no vellum copies.
Issued 29 October 1894.
NOTEs: On 8 March 1894 Bernard Quaritch wrote to Morris: Would you allow me to print at the Kelmscott Press a small volume: The Book of Wisdom and Lies[,] a Georgian storybook translated by Wardrop[.] The edition to be 25o, and you to undertake the supervision (not the proof-reading) to supply paper, reproduce a portrait etc.
I have the translators Manuscript'(HEH HM 36666).
Two days later Quaritch added: ‘I have sent the Ms. of the Georgian Tales to your address by parcel post.
There is no necessity of giving a portrait and the Arms of Georgia.
If you accept the printing of the Ms. it shall be with the understanding that neither I nor the translator interfere with you'(HEH HM 36667).
On 17 March Morris sent Quaritch three sets of two specimen pages (see Dr.6) with this note: ‘I shall be glad to print it for you, and will do 25o copies at £,1.5.o per copy delivered to you bound in wrapping vellum with silk strings in the manner of my Kelmscott Press books'(BQ).
Quaritch was pleased with the specimen pages and accepted Morris's terms on 19 March.
I think the volume will form an original and respectable link in the series of Kelmscott press publications,’he declared (HEH HM 36668).
Morris completed the design for the engraved title on 22 August 1894 (The Letters of William Morris to His Family and Friends, ed.
Philip Henderson [195o], p.
36o).
He purchased ten copies of the book for his own use (Cockerell to Quaritch, 1o October 1894 [LB]).
Cost of production was £277 2s.
8d.
(Ledger).

BINDING: Limp vellum.
Stamped in gold on spines: ‘SHELLEYS / POEMS / VOL.
I [11] [111] [Golden type].
PUBLICATION: 250 paper copies (25s. per volume); 6 vellum copies (8 gns. per volume).
Vol. i issued 29 November 1894; vol. ii issued 25 March 1895; vol. iii issued 28 November 1895.
NOTEs: Ellis wrote to Morris on 6 January 1894: I quite agree with you as to the doubt whether so big a book as Shelley ought to make.
'I esteem all his work good except his juvenilia (written before Q.[ueen] Mab.).
To make a volume of his shorter poems & lyrics would seem to me mean & unfair[.] But I doubt extremely if 3 fattish volumes would pay & I think perhaps therefore it is better to let him alone'(BL Add.
Ms.
52715, f.
11).
Ellis promised to consult Swinburne about the matter.
On 17 January Cockerell recorded the decision in his diary: ‘w.M. is going to print a Shelley.' The usual silk ties were not used in the Shelley.
The ties were omitted from the Shelley as an experiment, as some people don't care for them,’Cockerell wrote to Stopford Brooke on 14 December 1894, ‘but except in the 2 other volumes of this work, they will appear in all future books that are bound in vellum'(LB).
In a letter to the publishers Dodd, Mead & Co., 29 March 1895, Cockerell said, I have had no other complaints of the binding of the Shelley, though, as the volumes are thick, I understand the objection.
It is of course necessary to bind the 3 vols. uniformly' (LB).
The vellum copies, however, do have ties. ‘Some of the half-borders designed for The Wood beyond the World [A27] reappear before the longer poems' (‘List’).
Cost of production for the three volumes was £832 4s.
(Ledger).
On 22 March 1895 Cockerell sent to Leighton a list of those who were to receive complimentary copies of volume ii: Burne-Jones, Georgiana Burne-Jones, Philip Webb, Emery Walker, Miss Burden, Theodore Watts, Swinburne, Miss Kate Faulkner, May Sparling, F.
Smith (Morris & Co.), and R.
Smith (Morris & Co.) (LB).

## PSALMI
PENITENTIALES.
COLOPHON: THUS ends this rhymed version of the / Penitential Psalms [ornament 1] Found in a Manu-/script of Horæ Beatæ Mariæ Virginis, writ-/ten at Gloucester about the year 144o, and / now transcribed and edited by F.
S.
Ellis. / Printed by William Morris at the Kelms-/cott Press, 14, Upper Mall, Hammersmith. / Finished on the 15th day of November, 1894. / [printer's mark 1] COLLATION: $8 ^ { \mathrm { o } } : [ { \mathrm { a } } ] ^ { 4 } \ { \mathrm { b } } - { \mathrm { e } } ^ { 8 } .$ 36 leaves, pp. [8] [1] 2-63 [64].
PAPER & DIMENS1ONS: Flower(2) paper.
Leaf: 207 × 140 mm.
Text: 26 lines (including blank lines) × 88 mm.
TYPE: Chaucer type.
ORNAMENTs: Three-quarter borders; three- and six-line initials; Latin texts in red.
CoNTENs: [a]ı-[a]4 blank [a]4 title br-e6r text e6 colophon $\mathrm { e } 7 ^ { \mathrm { r } } { - } \mathrm { e } 8 ^ { \mathrm { r } }$ glossary e8v blank.

NOTEs: . . . the Rev.
Professor Skeat has pointed out that the scribe must have copied them from an older manuscript, as they are in the Kentish dialect of about a century earlier.
The half-border on p.
34 appears for the first time in this book' (List’).
Ellis made considerable alterations in the text and consulted Morris and F.
J.
Furnivall while editing the volume (see b below).
As usual, he and Morris disagreed about the editorial apparatus.
On 2 November 1894 Morris wrote to him: ‘Tis clear that I dont take the same view of a glossary as you do; I dont think we want a series of synonyms, but in each case the exact modern equivalent of the word, and the new word should be the nearest possible, e.g. not “rigid" for stef", but stiff.
In short all we want is some ½ dozen words like ord (Iclandic oddi) point, or thorns.
Otherwise we have something which a reader of old English doesn't want, and which is not enough for a non-reader, who would want every word respelt or p[a]raphrased.
Please excuse me; but you told me to knock the glossary about; now I have done so' (PML).
Cost of production was £,71 13s.
(Ledger).
By 4 October 1894 the entire edition was subscribed for (Cockerell to Messrs.
Cornish Bros. [LB]); hence it was not included in the κP list of ι December 1894 (C28).
The book evoked one of Mrs.
Morris's rare comments (in a letter to Blunt, 15 December [1894]): ‘Some small books are being printed at [the} same time [as the Chaucer], one is quite lovely, the penitential psalms in mediaeval verse - with a great deal of red – I fear it is all sold, those small things go off at once before one knows about them often'(FM[1]).

## A3ī
Girolamo Savonarola, Epistola de Contemptu Mundi Epistola de Contemptu Mundi di Frate / Hieronymo da Ferrara dellordine de frati / predicatori la quale manda ad Elena Buon-/accorsi sua madre, per consolarla della / morte del fratello, suo Zio. / [woodengraving] COLOPHON: [X7] [paragraph symbol] Questa edizione e la prima cavata / dall’autografo, da lungo tempo conser-/vato dalle monache del Malgio a Fir-/renze.
Seque parole per parola l’origi-/nale & l’ortografia dell’autore, ma / e stampata senza le abbreviazioni, / perche lo stampatore non possiede i/ segni che sarebbero necessari. / [paragraph symbol] Le parole stampate in rosso, sono / logore o mancanti nell’originale, cheha / subito i guasti del tempo & della / umidita, & sono state riportate duana / copia antica della lettera essistente a / Firenze, pubblicata dal Padre Vincen-/zo Marchesi nel 18so. / [paragraph symbol] La figura del Frate sulla prima pa-/gina e stata disegnata dalle’editore Car/lo Fairfax Murray & incisa in legno da / Guglielmo H.
Hooper.
Tutti gli ornati / sono stati disegnati da Guglielmo / Morris. / [paragraph symbol] Di questa edizione privata fatta a / spese delle’editore, sono state tirate / cento cinquanta copie in carta, & sei in / pergamena. / [χ8] [paragraph symbol] Impresso in London per Guglielmo / Morris alla Stamperia Kelmscott./ Adi ultimo di novembre Mdccclxxxxiv. / [printer's mark 1] COLLATION: 80: X8.
8 leaves, PP. [1-3] 4-15 [16].
PAPER & DIMENSIONS: Flower(2) paper.
Leaf: 207 × I40 mm.
Text: 27 lines ×79 mm.
TYPE: Chaucer type.
ORNAMENTS & ILLUSTRATION: Border I; six-line initial; conjecturally restored words and printer's mark in red; wood-engraved illustration designed by Charles Fairfax Murray on title-page.
CONTENTS: χI title χI blank χ2r-X7 text X7v- χ8 colophon χ8v blank.
BINDING: Quarter holland (blue paper on boards).
No title on front cover.
PUBLICATION: Iso paper copies; 6 vellum copies.
Not for sale.
Ready 12 December 1894.
NOTEs: Charles Fairfax Murray began working for Morris & Co. in the 187os as an artist and copyist (Introductions, i, 26o); for many years he maintained a close relationship with Morris, Ruskin, Rossetti, and Burne-Jones, and in the latter part of his life he became celebrated as a connoisseur and collector.
(See Carole Cable, Charles Fairfax Murray, Assistant to Dante Gabriel Rossetti', Library Chronicle of the University of Texas, NS 10 [1978], 81–9.) 'The little Psalmi Penitentiales,’said Morris, has not only an archaeological but a very high literary value' (Ideal Book, p.
1os).
Since the text is in Italian, there is an unusually large number of typographical errors in the proofs (see d below).
Cost of production was £,8 4s.
7d.
(Ledger).
On 28 December [1894] Ellis wrote to Murray, By no means the smallest value of the book is your lovely design on the title.
If I do not entirely give credence to demoniacal possession I shall hereafter be a believer in angelical possession for the spirit of Fra Angelico or one of his friends must have taken possession of your fingers when you drew that exquisite design, instinct with the feeling of the 1sth century' (PML).
Murray's wood-engraving was a close adaption of a woodcut in Epistola di Frate Hieronymo da Ferrara dellordine de frati predicatori a uno Amico (Florence, n.d. [late sixteenth century]).
Murray also drew a few details from another woodcut portrait of Savonarola at his writing desk which appears, with slight variations, in a number of works by Savonarola that were printed in Florence and Venice in the early sixteenth century.
The book was privately printed for Murray and was of course not for sale at the KP.
Hence it is probably the only kP book that was ever inscribed to Morris (see k below).

COLLATION: $4 ^ { \mathrm { { o } } } \colon [ \mathrm { a } ] ^ { 4 } ~ [ \mathrm { b } ] ^ { 8 } ~ \mathrm { c } - \mathrm { h } ^ { 8 } ~ \mathrm { i } ^ { 4 }$ 64 leaves, pp. [i-ii] iv-vi [2] [=8] [1] 2-119 [120].
PAPER & DIMENSIONS: Perch paper.
Leaf: 290 × 2IC mm.
Text: 32 lines × 130 mm (prelims and end matter) or 140 mm (text).
TYPE: Text in Troy type; shoulder and side notes, argument, list of persons and places, and glossary in Chaucer type.
ORNAMENTs: Borders 14a and 14; half and three-quarter borders; three-, six-, and eight-line initials; section titles and shoulder and side notes in red.
CoNTENTs: [a]1-[a]1ν blank [a]2r title [a]2ν-[a]3 argument [a]4r blank [a]4 engraved title [b]1r-h7 text h8r colophon h8v-i3r list of persons and places i3v-i4 glossary i4 blank.
BINDING: Limp vellum with silk \_ties.
Stamped in gold on spine: 'BEOWULF’ [enlarged Golden type].
PUBLICATION: 300 paper copies (2 gns.); 8 vellum copies (£Io).
Issued 2 February 1895.
NOTEs: Morris said of Beowulf that it was ‘the first and the best poem of the English race, [with] no author but the people' (A WS, ii, 494).
On 21 February 1893 Morris began a translation bāsed on a paraphrase by Alfred John Wyatt, the Anglo-Saxon scholar (List’).
Four days later Morris recorded in his diary: Finished the first lot of Beo: about 70o lines.
Wrote Wyatt.' The later history of the translation can be traced in Cockerell's diary.
1o April 1894: ‘w.M. finished the Beowulf.’11 April 1894: ‘Mr Wyatt at K.[elmscott] H.[ouse] to go over the Beowulf with W.M.'23 June 1894: A J Wyatt at K.H. with w.M. revising the Beowulf.’ 25 June 1894: Looking through proofs of the Beowulf ... all day.'1o December 1894: ‘w.M. wrote the argument for the Beowulf, which was finally passed for press by him & Wyatt.' 4 February 1895: Beowulf published.'(Observe that this differs from the official date of publication given in the ‘List’.) ‘The borders in the book were only used once again, in the Jason [A34]'(‘List’).
Cost of production was £485 4s.
4d.
(Ledger).
Morris claimed that he lost money on the book: It was announced in the prospectus that it would be published at two guineas but in the printing of the book several sheets got spoiled and had to be reprintêd.
The mere cost of reprinting these sheets and the expense of the extra paper coverted my profit into a loss – and the book is sold at less than what it cost me to produce it’ (Ideal Book, p.
111).
The translation by Morris and Wyatt was published by Longmans in August 1898.

The story of Sir Perceval exists in several mediaeval manuscripts and is referred to by Chaucer in the tale of Sir Thopas.
Aside from beginning each section with an initial rather than a number, the KP edition appears to be an exact reprint.
On 8 February 1895 Cockerell wrote to Charles F.

Burne Jones, & though it is nearly printed, this will delay it' (LB).

The book was delivered to the binder on 16 April (Cockerell to Leighton, 9 April 1895 [LB]).
Burne-Jones was paid £2o for designing the frontispiece; cost of production was £113 15s.
1d.
(Ledger).

NOTEs: Work began on the book in March 1894 (Ledger).
Cockerell wrote to Elkin Matthews on I October 1894, It [Jason] is nearly all in type, but it won't be ready for some time, as Burne-Jones has not yet set to work on the 2 illustrations'(LB).
Burne-Jones delivered the designs on 21 January 1895 (Morris diary), and on 6 June the book went to the binder (Cockerell diary).
Jason, which was originally intended to be part of The Earthly Paradise (Introductions, i, 42), was first published in 1867 and was revised by Morris for the 1868, 1882, and 1895 editions.
Morris admitted to an interviewer in 1895 that the KP edition must have been priced too high, since the book· had not sold well (Ideal Book, p.
113).
The line-fillings on the last page were cut on metal for this book, and cast like type (List’).
Burne-Jones was paid £4o for his designs; cost of production was £338 15s.
6d.
(Ledger).

NoTEs: The story – whose plot was suggested by that of Havelok the Dane, printed by the Early English Text Society'(‘List’).- was first begun in verse by Morris on 4 February 1895.
(A single leaf of that manuscript, rescued by Cockerell when Morris attempted to light his pipe with it, is in HEH HM 36919.) According to May Morris, ‘When finished the tale turned out to be so much longer than intended for a small book that, to meet the additional expense of production, the issue was increased to more than double the usual number.
But for an edition of six hundred my father thought the price (fifteen shillings) so high that, to add to the attraction, he gave notice that he undertook not to reprint it in any cheaper edition' (Introductions, ii, soo-1).
In an interview in 1895, Morris said, When you come to look at it, and see that you get a book like this . . . for seven and sixpence a vol-. ume, it would be impossible to refuse buying them.
I love my books, and I love making them, and I think these little octavos [sic] quite the most charming things issued from my press' (Ideal Book, p.
