## A49
Sydney C.
Cockerell (ed.), Some German Woodcuts of the Fifteenth Century

## SOME
GERMAN WOODCUTS OF THE FIFTEENTH / CENTURY COLOPHON: [[e]6r] Here ends Some German Woodcuts of the Fifteenth Century, for / which the blocks (with one exception) were prepared by Walker / and Boutall under the direction of the late William Morris.
Now / edited by S.
C.
Cockerell, and printed at the Kelmscott Press, Upper / Mall, Hammersmith.
Finished on the 1sth day of December, 1897. / [[e]6v] [printer's mark 2] / Sold by the Trustees of the late William Morris at the Kelmscott Press.
FOREwORD: Of the 35 reproductions of German woodcuts here given, 29 are all / that were done of a series chosen by William Morris to appear in / the Catalogue of his library, which was to have been annotated by / him and printed at the Kelmscott Press.
The other 6 were made for / his article in the 4th number of Bibliographica on the Early Wood-/cut Books of Ulm & Augsburg, and thanks are due to Messrs.
Ke-/gan Paul, Trench, Trübner & Co. for the loan of the blocks, as well / as for permission to quote the portions of the article which serve as / an introduction to this book.
These quotations will show the nature / of the remarks that Mr.
Morris intended to write for the Catalogue, / of which they would have been the most interesting feature.
A minor / feature was to have been a statement of the number of different cuts, / and the number of cuts including repetitions, in the principal illus-/trated books.
For this purely scientific side of the work I was made / responsible, and as the early printers usually repeated their cuts, and / no one has hitherto troubled to count them in this way, it has seemed / worth while to preserve these few statistics now that the original de-/sign of the Catalogue has fallen through.
They are accordingly print-/ed as an appendix in a list of the more important woodcut books that / the library contained.
For some particulars in this section I am / indebted to my friend Mr.
Robert Proctor, who has kindly looked / over the proofsheets, and pointed out errors and omissions. / [paragraph] I have been tempted to add a list of the manuscripts, which, though / comparatively few in number (112) were the most notable part of the / library; but without a full description of each volume such a list would / be meaningless, and anything more than a list would be out of place. / S.
C.
CÔCKERELL.

PAPER & DIMENSIONS: Perch paper.
Leaf: 290 × 2I3 mm.

TYPE: Golden type.
ORNAMENTS & ILLUSTRATIONS: Six-line initial; titles, marginal figure references, first paragraph of colophon, and some words in red; 35 line-block illustrations.
CoNTENTs: π title πı blank ar foreword (by Cockerell) ai blank a2r-a4 introduction (by Morris) a4v-π2r A LIST OF THE WOODCUTS OF THE FIFTEENTH / CENTURY REPRO-DUCED IN THIS BOOK.'π2 blank [b]r-[d]7 illustrations [d]- 7v blank [d]8r-[e]5 [ornament 3] A LIST OF THE PRINCIPAL BOOKS OF THE / FIFTEENTH CENTURY, CONTAIN-ING WOOD-/CUTS, IN THE LIBRARY OF THE LATE WILLIAM / MORRIS.

AND REFERENCES TO HAIN'S RE-PER-/TORIUM BIBLIOGRAPHICUM.'[e]5-[e]6 ANAL-YSIS OF THE WOODCUTS IN THE LATIN / EDITION OF THE NUREMBERG CHRONICLE.' [e]6 colophon [e]6v printer's mark and remainder of colophon.
BINDiNG: Quarter holland (blue paper on boards).
Printed in black on front cover: ‘SOME GERMAN WOODCUTS OF THE FIFTEENTH CENTURY' [Golden type].
PUBLICATION: 225 paper copies (3os.); 8 vellum copies (5 gns.).
Issued 6 January 1898.
NOTEs: Morris asked Cockerell ‘to help him to catalogue his library' on the evening of 19 October 1892 (Cockerell diary), and for the next few years Cockerell, in addition to his other duties, studied and catalogued Morris's extensive collection of incunabula and mediaeval manuscripts.
Out of this activity grew Morris's scheme of a heavily illustrated catalogue of his library, with descriptions by Cockerell and additional notes by himself.
To an interviewer in 1895, Morris said, ‘This will be largely illustrated with facsimiles of the plates and pages of the book themselves.
Of course, this is just a little fancy for my own pleasure, and yet I have an idea that there ought to be many book-lovers and art-lovers who would find much in the volume to delight them. . . .
You see that I am sparing no trouble to give the finest reproductions’ (Ideal Book, p.
112).
Two trial pages of the projected catalogue have survived (BL C.43.- f.24 [∆b12] and C.102.h.18).
The first is a description of a psalter, N.E.
France, c.
126o, on a leaf of Flower paper (281 × 204 mm), with a text of 33 lines × 127 mm.
The other is a corrected proof of a description of Speculum Humanae Salvationis (1473), with a text of 35 lines × 132 mm.
A number of manuscript notes by Morris for the catalogue – which he began on 21 May 1894 (Cockerell diary) – are extant, most plentifully in the Morgan Library.
Morris planned to charge about five guineas for the catalogue (Cockerell to Macmillan & Bowes, 24 December 1895 [LB]).
After his death the book had to be abandoned (but not before £ı7o Is.
4d. had been expended on it [Ledger]), though for a time there was talk of printing it elsewhere (Cockerell to Robert Proctor, 9 November 1896 (BL Add.
Ms 52743, f.
238]).
Instead Cockerell decided to combine the illustrations of woodcuts that had already been prepared (all but one by Walker & Boutall) with portions of an article by Morris, On the Artistic Qualities of the Woodcut Books of Ulm and Augsburg in the Fifteenth Century', Bibliographica, 1 (1895), 437-55.
The book sold briskly before publication. ‘There has been a great rush on the German woodcuts & they were all sold out last week,' Cockerell wrote to Leighton on 3o November 1897. . . .
A good many people have been left out in the cold, as orders keep coming in!
I suppose it is the announcement that the Press is closing that has made people so eager'(JC).
In another letter to Leighton, 14 December 1897, Cockerell said, At the last moment I have discovered a tiresome misprint in the colophon of ‘Some German woodcuts’- which necessitates a cancel.
This will be finished tomorrow & can be fetched on Friday' (JC).

## Aso
William Morris, The Story of Sigurd the Volsung THE STORY OF SIGURD THE VOLSUNG AND THE / FALL OF THE NIBLUNGS [ornament 1] BY WILLIAM MOR-RIS [Troy type] CoLopHoN: Here ends The Story of Sigurd the Volsung and the Fall of the Niblungs, written / by William Morris.
With two pictures designed by Edward Burne-Jones and / engraved by W.
H.
Hooper.
It was printed at the Kelmscott Press, Upper Mall, / Hammersmith, and finished on the 19th day of January, 1898. / [paragraph] Sold by the Trustees of the late William Morris at the Kelmscott Press.
COLLATION: Small 20: a⁴ b−08 [p]².
110 leaves, PP. [8] [1] 2-207 [208– 10] [2] [=212].
PAPER & DIMENSIONS: Apple paper.
Leaf: 328 × 23I mm.
Text: 48 lines × 169 mm.
TYPE: Text in Chaucer type; titles of books in Troy type.
ORNAMENTS & ILLUSTRATIONS: Borders 33a and 33; side and corner borders; three-, four-, six-, and ten-line initials; section titles and shoulder titles in red; wood-engraved frontispiece and illustration on [p]ɪ (p. [2o9]) designed by Burne-Jones.
CONTENTS: aI-aI blank a2 title a2 blank a3-a3 table of contents a4 blank a4 frontispiece bır-o8 text [p]ı illustration [p]1 colophon [p]1-[p]2ν blank.
BINDING: Limp vellum with silk ties.
Stamped in gold on spine: ‘SIGURD' [enlarged Golden type].
PUBLICATION: 160 paper copies (6 gns.); 6 vellum copies (20 gns.).
Issued 25 February 1898.
NOTEs: Sigurd was first published in 1877.
The earliest references to the KP edition are, as usual, in Cockerell's diary.
5 November 1891: ‘Saw . . . a trial page of Sigurd. . . .’ 2o October 1892: ‘Sigurd to be illustrated (perhaps) by B.[urne-]J.[ones] but cannot be thought of till the Chaucer [A40] is finished.'21 October 1895: ‘w.M. full of the proposed new folio Sigurd with B-J illustrations.' However, only with great reluctance did Burne-Jones agree to illustrate the book. ‘To pacify and please Morris I will do what I can to adorn the Volsung Tale,' he wrote to Lady Leighton in late 1895 (Memorials, ii, 276). ‘I shan't like doing it so much as Chaucer,’Burne-Jones commented to his studio assistant on 7 January 1896. ‘The scenery for one thing isn't quite a painter's scenery – it's more a poet's’ (Rooke diary).
On 5 May 1896 he complained, It's quite wonderful how distasteful the task of making drawings of it is to me. . ..
Terrible the tragedy of it is, and the fine things in it are so much fitter for literature than for my art, and I've no turn for the dramatic'(Rooke diary).
As a result Burne-Jones made slow progress on the illustrations (which were to number about twenty-five), and in any case Morris's death meant the abandonment of the plans for a fully illustrated folio.
Thirty-two copies of two folio pages (see Dr.9) were privately distributed to friends of Morris in January 1897.
On the evening of 26 January 1897 Cockerell persuaded Burne-Jones to supply two illustrations for a more modest edition (Cockerell diary).
Catterson-Smith redrew the designs (Cockerell diary, 6 October 1897).
The two borders used in this book were almost the last that Mr.
Morris designed.
They were intended for an edition of The Hill of Venus, which was to have been written in prose by him and illustrated by Sir E.
Burne-Jones.
The foliage was suggested by the ornament in two Psalters of the last half of the thirteenth century in the library at Kelmscott House.
The initial A at the beginning of the 3rd book was designed in March, 1893, for the Froissart [A4o], and does not appear elsewhere'(‘List’).
RELATED MATERIALS: (a) Intermediate drawing for second illustration (wmG).
(b) Four preliminary drawings by Burne-Jones probably intended for Sigurd (v & A[2] 1.11o).
(c) ‘Original designs for [five] initials intended for the large folio Sigurd the Volsung.
A and B appear for the only time on these proofs.

Cribb. coNTENTs: [a]1−[a]3ν blank [a]4 title [a]4ν blank bır−2k6r text 2k6 colophon.
BıNDıNG: Quarter holland (blue paper on boards).
Printed in black on paper label on spine: ‘THE / SUNDERING / FLOOD / BY / WILLIAM / MORRIS'[Golden type]; printed in black on front cover: ‘THE SUNDERING FLOOD.
WRITTEN / BY WIL-LIAM MORRIS' [Chaucer type].

He began to write it on Dec.
21, 1895, and dictated the final words [to Cockerell] on Sept.
8, 1896'(List’).
A trial page was printed on 19 May 1897 (Cockerell diary).
In a letter to N.
L.
McMinn, 13 February 1928 (κM), Emery Walker explained how his firm, Walker & Boutali, produced the map on the front pastedown of The Sundering Flood and similar maps in the volumes of the Saga Library: either Morris or Magnússon, he said, would submit a rough sketch that included the names of places.
An employee of Walker & Boutall – in this case H.
Cribb – then drew a finished design, and a line block was made from it.
The original plan was to place the map in the book proper, as Cockerell reported to Jenny Morris, 28 February 1898: . . . one or two find fault with the map being pasted inside the cover of the Sundering Flood.
I knew this would provoke complaints, but it was done after much consideration, as the map was found not to harmonise in style, scale, or colour with the first page of the book, opposite to which it had been intended to put it'(BL Add.
MS.
52739, f.
52).
Sheet b was reprinted because a border used on it was found to be unsatisfactory (see c below).
For a discusssion of the emendations made by May Morris in the KP text, see Introductions, ii, 621-2.
A less expensive edition of The Sundering Flood was published by Longmans in February 1898.

UNFINISHED BOOKS This category includes books that were planned and announced but eventually aborted and, in other cases, books that were mentioned by Morris but never really begun.
The books that were more seriously contemplated by Morris are placed first in the list.

## B1
Froissart's Chronicles See NOTEs for A46 and D1.8.

## B2
Shakespeare's Plays On the fyleaf of a copy of The Heimskringla (1893) is the following note in the hand of Frederick J.
Furnivall, founder of the New Shakspere Society: ‘F.
J.
Furnivall, from Wm.
Morris, at his Kelmscott House, Upr.
Mall, Hammersmith, on Sunday evg., 26 Feb.
1893, when he assented (provisionally) to F.J.F.'s proposal that he should print a grand Shakspere in 3 vols. folio, edited by P.
A.
Daniel, or F.J.F., after his Chaucer (ed.
F.
S.
Ellis) was done'(jDc).
On 23 April 1893 Cockerell recorded in his diary, wm is to print a big Shakespeare for Longman.'However, in a letter to Frank E.
Bliss, 23 July 1894, Cockerell observed that‘the Shakespeare will not be ready for some time' (LB).
In the British Library (C.1o2.1.22) there is a trial page on Perch paper of two columns of Golden type; shoulder titles, speakers' names, and stage directions are in red.
Each column is 61 lines × 1o4 mm.
The passage printed is Macbeth, Act 1, Scene viii.
(Two copies of a slightly different trial page are known to be extant: one is in the Berger collection and one appeared in the Saks sale, no.
146.
A trial page similar to the one in the Berger collection is reproduced as the frontispiece in Books and Letters Collected by William Harris Arnold of New York [Jamaica, N.Y., 1901].) By 1894 it was decided to issue the Shakespeare in eight or ten 8º volumes at about 25/- or 3o/- per volume.
On 5 February 1895 Cockerell wrote to F.
E.
Thompson: ‘The Shakespeare is not yet in the press, as the Chaucer is absorbing all Mr.
Morris’attention.
It (the Sh.) will be a size between the Beowulf & the 8vo books unless present plans are altered’ (LB).
It is not surprising that Morris never found time to complete the Shakespeare.
He once admitted to Cockerell, Shakespeare did not much attract me, as I have not much sympathy with the dramatic form' (Introductions, ii, 677).

## B3
Vitas Patrum F.
S.
Ellis, the editor of this projected volume, wrote in an announcement (Cıs) issued in about February 1894: ‘This work, written by St.
Jerome in the fourth century, was translated into English by William Caxton during the last years of his life, and is stated by his servant and successor to have been finished on the day of his death. ‘It was printed at Wynkyn de Worde's press in 1495. . . . ‘Wynken de Worde's edition, which has never been reprinted, has become so rare that a fine perfect copy of the book has been sold for as much as two hundred and fifty pounds, and can rarely be met with even at that price.
It is a book of very great interest alike to the philologist, the hagiologist, and the student of folk-lore. ‘It is proposed to proceed with the printing of a new edition uniform with that of Caxton's Golden Legend, issued from the Kelmscott Press in 1892, as soon as the name of one hundred and fifty subscribers have been received.
In no case will more than two hundred copies be printed.
It will make two large 4to volumes, and the price will be five guineas.' Cockerell wrote to Charles F.
Carter on 7 August 1895: ‘Mr.
Morris has abandoned the idea of printing “Vitas Patrum", as the number of subscriptions did not justify his going on with the book' (LB).
For a specimen page, see D1.5.

## B4
Theodore Watts, The Coming of Love In a preface to the first and second editions of The Coming of Love (1898), Theodore Watts (afterwards Watts-Dunton) wrote: ‘Had it not been for the intervention of matters of a peculiarly absorbing kind – matters which caused me to delay the task of collecting these verses - I should have been the most favoured man who ever brought out a volume of poems, for they would have been printed by William Morris, at the Kelmscott Press.' Parts of The Coming of Love had appeared in the Athenaeum and other periodicals, but some of the poems had been lent and lost; hence another reason for the delay in publication.
The kP edition of 2oo copies was originally planned for publication in 1894 at two guineas, to appear simultaneously with John Lane's cheaper edition (Thomas Hake and Arthur Compton-Rickett, The Life and Letters of Theodore Watts-Dunton [1916], i, 276).
Even after Morris's death Cockerell called on Watts and Swinburne to discuss the book on 19 October 1896 (Cockerell diary), and the following day Cockerell drafted a memorandum on the subject (see E8).

## B5
A Catalogue of William Morris's Library See NOTES for A49.

## B6
Cupid and Psyche According to Georgiana Burne-Jones, One of many plans now ended [with the death of Morris in 1896] had been to publish an illustrated book of The Hill of Venus.
It was to have been rewritten and have had ornamental borders made for it by Morris; the pictures, redrawn, were those designed by Edward long ago for The Earthly Paradise.
The question as to whether the story should be written in verse or prose was settled by Morris saying, “Well, you know, old chap, prose looks blacker in the page and fills up better – so it's to be prose" (Memorials, ii, 290).
However, after calling on Burne-Jones on 8 December 1896, Cockerell recorded in his diary: EB-J talked about continuing the Kelmscott Press – & wanted his Cupid and Psyche drawings to be recut & printed.'The plan was that Catterson-Smith would, as usual, redraw the designs on photographic copies, and Hooper would cut the new blocks; the wood-engravings would be accompanied by the text of the Cupid and Psyche’section of The Earthly Paradise, set in Troy type.
Seven specimen quarto pages were printed at the kp in January 1897, and it was arranged, according to a note by Cockerell, ‘that the book should be printed after the close of the Kelmscott Press'.
The text area was to be 159 × 105 mm (HRC: Emery Walker papers).
A memorandum between Morris's trustees, Burne-Jones, and Longmans, Green (who were to publish the volume) is dated February 1897 (wMG K.774).
The project was finally abandoned after Burne-Jones's death in 1898.

## B7
The Bible In a letter to Blunt, 4 December 189o, discussing her husband's plans for printing, Jane Morris wrote, a nice bible is much needed, but the Universities only have the right to print that'(FM[1]). ‘He [Morris] has thought of printing . . . the Bible,' Cockerell noted in his diary on 1o September 1891.
Mackail, in a letter to Cockerell, 6 March 1899, recalled that Morris first thought of reprinting the Authorised Version but later favoured Wycliffe's translation (BL Add MS.
52734, f.
23).
Cockerell wrote to James G.
Clarke on 3 October 1894, Mr.
Morris has sometimes spoken of printing the Bible, but it would probably be in folio, & in any case it is too big an undertaking to be thought of until the Chaucer & Shakespeare are finished'(LB).
Several extant Burne-Jones drawings, illustrating episodes in the Bible and the Apocrypha, may have been intended for this project (WMA B, p.
11; Kenneth A.
Lohf, The Burne-Jonesiness of Burnejones’, Gazette of the Grolier Club, Ns No.
17 [May 1972], Pp.
46–70).

## B8
Gesta Romanorum ‘The black letter fount is almost ready, and he [Morris] thinks of making a beginning with John Ball.
Chaucer to follow – also perhaps “Gesta Romanorum"’(Cockerell diary, 22 October 1891).
Morris owned several editions of the work (Library, nos.
415-16, 888-9).

## B9
Some English Mediaeval Songs and Music So listed by Cockerell (List’).
The book of mediaeval English poetry I remember myself discussing with him [Morris] (or rather, taking part in a discussion between him & Burne-Jones) as early as 1892.
But it was one of the things that he repeatedly took up & laid aside again (Mackail to Cockerell, 6 March 1899 [BL Add.
Ms.
52734, f.
23]).

## B1o
John Bunyan, The Pilgrim's Progress w.M. talked of printing The Pilgrim's Progress (at Bernard Shaw's suggestion)'(Cockerell diary, 1o December 1894).

## Bı
A Book of Ballads Mr.
F.
S.
Ellis at K.[elmscott] H.[ouse.] Talk about a Book of Ballads to be printed at the Kelmscott Press'(Cockerell diary, 26 January 1894). ‘He [Morris] was engaged on the selection of the Ballads, which he spoke of as the finest poems in our language, during his last illness' (‘List’).

## B12
The Douce Apocalypse On 3 September (misdated August) 1894 Emery Walker wrote, on behalf of Morris, to E.
W.
Nicholson, Librarian of the Bodleian Library, requesting permission to photograph and publish in facsimile a thirteenth-century apocalypse (Ms.
Douce 18o) known as the Douce Apocalypse (Bod.: Applications to Facsimile, 1889-94).
On 28 November 1894 Walker and Morris spent the day at the Bodleian examining the manuscript, which had been a favourite with Morris when he was an undergraduate (Bod., L.P. entry book; note by Walker on letter from Morris to Walker, [3o October 1894] [íRC]).

## B13
The Musical Compositions of Henry vIII “Amongst other editions which he (Morris) hoped to have printed," says “The Saturday Review,"“was a volume of musical compositions by King Henry vı1. and the English composers contemporary with him.
The project originated with Sir Edward Burne-Jones, who was to have designed the woodcuts; and the music, which was to have consisted of the contents of two Mss. in the British Museum, Add.
Mss.
31,922 and 5,665, was to have been edited by Mr.
Arnold Dolmetsch.
The project was carried so far that the photographing of the Ms. was actually commenced"'(Aymer Vallance, William Morris [1897], p.
414).

## B14
Edward FitzGerald, The Rubáiyát of Omar Khayyám Cockerell's diary reveals that he suggested this title to Morris on at least three occasions.
11 June 1891: Suggested that Morris should print decent editions of Omar Khayyam and of“On the Nature of Gothic" from the Stones of Venice.
Morris seemed to think that he would do so, as The Golden Legend is such a big book that he will have to do some little ones after it is finished.’21 April 1892: “Suggested that WM should print a collection of his best loved poems including Omar Khayyam.’1 November 1892: I begged him to printOmar Khayyam, and I think he will do it.
In 1872 Morris, with the assistance of Burne-Jones and Fairfax Murray, had produced an illuminated Ms of The Rubáiyát (now in BL Add.
MS.
37832).

## B15
Scott's Novels He [Morris] has thought of printing Scott's novels as well as Chaucer and the Bible, after he has completed printing the Golden Legend and all his own books!!'(Cockerell diary, 1o September 1891).

## B16
Dickens'Novels In a letter to Frances Horner, probably written in November 1893, Burne-Jones said, . . .
Morris vows he will print Dickens (if he lives) at the Kelmscott Press' (FMʃ1]).
Morris hinted at something like this to an interviewer in 1895 (Ideal Book, p.
107).

## B17
John Ruskin, Unto This Last Morris declared his willingness to print “Unto This Last" at the Kelmscott Press . . .'(Cockerell diary, 24 March 1892).
However, in a letter to Morris of 2o January 1893, George Allen, Ruskin's publisher, rejected as too expensive Morris's terms of £i per copy for an edition of 300 copies (HEH HM 36676).

## B18
William Morris, The House of the Wolfings C.
M.
Gere, another nice young Birmingham artist, called in the aftn. with some drawings, and w.M. asked him to have a shot at illustrating the Wolfings’(Cockerell diary, 12 December 1892).
During 1893 and 1894 Gere produced a series of drawings, which Morris criticised in some detail.
(His letters to Gere are in the Huntington Library.) Trial pages in Chaucer and Troy types are extant (BC; BL C.102.h.18; BL C.43.f.24 [∆b12]), as is a design for a border (BL C.- 43.c.26 [ΣQQ46]).
Gere's drawings, annotated by Morris, are in a private collection in England.

## B19
William Morris, The Roots of the Mountains Morris intended to have A.
J.
Gaskin illustrate it (Cockerell diary, 23 April 1893).
A trial page in Chaucer type was struck off (BL C.102.h.18; BL C.43.f.24 [∆b12]).

## B2o
Poems by Ralph Waldo Emerson Late in 1894 F.
Holland Day, of the Boston publishing firm of Copeland & Day, approached Morris about printing a selection of Emerson's poems, which would be co-published by Copeland & Day in America.
In a letter to Day of ı January 1895, Cockerell outlined Morris's terms: 16/- per paper copy and £3 1os. per vellum copy for an edition of 3oo or 35o (LB).
But in the meantime Morris had agreed to print Hand and Soul (A36) for another American publisher; hence on 27 June 1895 Cockerell wrote to Day again, saying, I fear that there is now very little chance of the Emerson being begun this year, other things having been taken in hand'(LB).

## B21
Others For the following titles I have found no documentation other than citation by Cockerell in his ‘List’.
1.
The High History of the San Graal, translated by Sebastian Evans.
2.
Piers Ploughman.
3.
Huon of Bordeaux.
4.
Caxton's Jason.
5.
A Latin Psalter.
6.
The Prymer or Lay Folk's Prayer-Book.
7.
Malory's Morte Darthur.
ADVERTISING CIRCULARS During the first year of its existence the κP did not advertise its books, probably because most of them were fully subscribed by the date of publication.
As Cockerell explained later, ‘We only send out special announcements of books which are not nearly sold out when they go to the binder' (Cockerell to W.
Glaisher, 24 October 1895 [LB]).
The circulars proved to be effective: Cockerell noted in his diary on 1 October 1894, for example: Lots of k.P. orders owing to circular sent out on Friday.'The first of the lists to include titles ‘in preparation’, that of 1 December 1892 (C3), was prompted by an inaccurāte report in a London newspaper, and it ‘was necessary', said Morris, ‘to clear up the matter by a rational statement of what books were in preparation' (Morris to Quaritch, 1 December 1892 [BQ]).
In the following entries the circulars are printed on leaves of the Flower paper of approximately 21o × 143 mm unless otherwise indicated.
The Whatman paper used for a few of the earliest circulars was stock left over from the large-paper edition of The Roots of the Mountains (189o).
Title numbers not accompanied by a letter are titles listed in Section A; italic numbers indicate titles announced for the first time.
Unless otherwise noted, the circulars are printed in black ink only; ‘two colours' means both black and red ink are used.
The Morris & Co. lists were distributed directly by Morris & Co. to their customers rather than by the KP.

## C51
Announcement of Love is Enough (A52) and A Note by William Morris (A53), 25 February 1898 (‘Specimens', no.
168.) One leaf.
Golden type.
Proof: BL C.43.c.26 (ΣQ22).

Also this notice: ‘Three hand-presses and the remaining plant of the Kelmscott Press (exclusive of the type and ornaments), will be sold by private treaty.
Particulars may be obtained from S.
C.
Cockerell at 14, Upper Mall, Hammersmith, W.' This final circular was issued in several forms, at least one of which was printed on Perch paper (Jc).
In the earlier issues more books (especially vellum copies) were listed, and the announcement about the presses was not included; these issues were essentially proofs, probably sent to only a few booksellers.
Cockerell's copy of an early issue bears this note: ‘12 pulls taken Jan 18 1898'(CL ‘Morrisiana 18’).
D.
EPHEMERA Though the kP produced a surprisingly large amount of ephemera, Morris in general disliked job printing.
On 23 July 1894, for instance, Cockerell wrote to Larner Sugden: I regret that Mr.
Morris has decided not to print any leaflets in future.
He has done it once or twice to oblige personal friends, but it was found to interfere too much with the other work'(LB).
And to F.
C.
Eden, on 25 September 1894, Cockerell reported that Morris ‘regrets that he does not see his way to printing the Creed, Lord's Prayer & the Commandments in the manner proposed in your letter of the 7th inst.
He feels that the printing press is essentially for the multiplication of copies, & that when a single copy only is required it is much better to get it written out or painted' (LB).
On the same date Morris, again through Cockerell, declined to print a syllabus for T.
Edwin Ashworth, suggesting that it be printed instead at the Chiswick Press (LB).

## D1
Specimen Pages This category does not include trial pages’ prepared at the KP as an aid in designing books; by ‘specimen pages' is meant pages that were distributed to booksellers, customers and friends of the Press, or the publisher of the book in question.
See also A46, which is included in Šection A rather than here because it was in fact published.
1.
The Recuyell of the Historyes of Troye (A8), four pages, including the explicit (p.
1), Caxton's prologue (pp.
2-4), and Morris's colophon and printer's mark 1 (p.
4).
(Copy examined: Bod.
Kelmscott Press d.4.) Troy type.
Eight-line initials, and corner and side borders.
Last two lines on first page in red.
Two leaves of Flower paper, each 274 × 193 mm (trimmed).
Text: 33 lines × 13o mm.
An entry in Proctor's notebook indicates that it was a ‘specimen printed for Quaritch [the publisher] 1892’.
Quaritch ordered 2ooo copies of the specimen (Saks sale, no.
181[24]).
2.
The History of Godefrey of Boloyne (A15), one page (the beginning of chap.
CC).
(Specimens’, no.
8.) Troy type.
Eight-line initial and three-quarter border.
Chapter title in red.
One leaf of Flower paper, 287 × 204 mm.
Text: 31 lines × 125 mm.
Both this and the following specimen page accompanied the Godefrey announcement (Cs), which was probably issued in April 1893.
2a.
The History of Godefrey of Boloyne (A15), p.
415 (beginning ‘must neds take it on hym . . .’).
(‘Specimens’, no.
7.) Troy type.
Six-line initials and side border.
One leaf of Flower paper, 283 × 2o8 mm.
Text: 32 lines × 125 mm.
3.
Sidonia the Sorceress (A19), p.
12.
(Specimens', no.
I1.) Golden type.

The Tale of King Florus and the Fair Jehane (A21), p.
85.
(Specimens', no.
12.) Chaucer type.
Three-line initial and shoulder title (the latter in red).
Text: 22 lines × 64 mm.
This appears on the second page of the King Florus announcement (C) (probably issued in December 1893), of which soo copies were printed.
5.
Vitas Patrum (B3), p.
75.
(‘Specimens’, no.
21.) Golden type.
Sixline initial and shoulder title.
One leaf of Flower paper, 29o × 207 mm.
Text: 42 lines × 125 mm.
This accompanied 50o copies of the prospectus of Vitas Patrum (C15), which was probably issued in late February 1894, though Vitas Patrum itself was never published.
6.
The Book of Wisdom and Lies (A28), pp.
11-12.
(Copies examined: BL C.1o2.h.18; BQ.) Golden type.
Six-line initials.
Shoulder notes and titles in red.
Two leaves of Flower paper, each 204× 140 mm.
Text: 30 lines × 88 mm.
Morris sent three copies of these pages to Bernard Quaritch, the publisher of the book, on 17 March 1894 (BQ), and Quaritch replied on 19 March that he was ‘delighted with the specimen leaves'(HEH HM 36668).
7.
The Works of Geoffrey Chaucer (A4o), pp.
I-2.
(‘Specimens', no.
73.) Chaucer type.
Border, illustration by Burne-Jones with frame, nineteen-line initial word, and six-line initial.
One leaf of Perch paper, 423 × 285 mm.
Text (p.
2): two columns of 63 lines × 105 mm each.
The word ‘SPECIMÊN.’ is printed in red (Golden type) in the lower left corner of p.
1.
The first page differs slightly from that of the published version in that the left column contains seven rather than six lines.
The specimen pages were printed in August 1894 even though all paper copies, and all but three vellum copies, of the Chaucer had already been sold.
On 3o October 1894 Cockerell wrote to Allan F.
Vigers: I regret to inform you that . . . the very few specimen pages struck off have been distributed among the leading booksellers' (LB).
When Quaritch later asked for twenty-five copies of the specimen (after Morris had decided to print an additional ioo copies of the Chaucer), Cockerell replied on 19 November 1894: ‘The few specimens of the first sheet of the Chaucer struck off were distributed long ago, but as soon as we are printing another picture I will have an extra 25 done for you' (LB).
8.
Froissart's Chronicles (B1), 16 pages.
(Copy examined: Bod.) Text in Chaucer type; title on first page in Troy type.
Three- and ten-line initials (many of which are left blank) and half-border.
Eight leaves of Perch paper, each $4 2 0 \times 2 8 4$ mm.
Text: two columns of 63 lines × 87 mm each.
The colophon reads: Incomplete sheets of Froissart.
32 copies printed at the Kelmscott Press on Dec.
24, / 1896, before the distribution of the type.
Not for sale.' This is a fuller version of the specimen sold at the KP $\left( \mathbf { A } _ { 4 } 6 \right)$ : the first and second pages are identical to [a]2 and [a]ı, respectively, of the latter, except that this privately printed version contains fewer ornaments.
Proof of p.
1: Sotheby cat., 18 December 1944, no.
195.
9.
Sigurd the Volsung (Aso), two pages (the second of which is p.
19).
(Copy examined: Bod.) Text in Troy type; colophon in Chaucer type.
Border, eight-line initials, blank space for fourteen-line initial word.
Colophon in red.
Two leaves of Perch paper, each 42o × 28o mm.
Text: 49 lines × 19o mm.
The second and third pages are blank.
The colophon reads: [ornament 1] Incomplete sheet of Sigurd the Volsung.
32 copies printed at the Kelmscott Press on / Jan.
11, 1897, before the distribution of the type.
Not for sale.' Cockerell's diary indicates that the pages were issued on 22 January 1897.
The pages record the form in which Morris originally intended to print Sigurd; they were distributed to friends along with the Froissart specimen pages (above).

## D2
Press Stationery and Forms The majority of these items are undatable, but many of them appear to have been printed after Sydney Cockerell became Secretary of the Press in July 1894.
Before that date, for example, Sparling seems to have carried on the kP correspondence on his personal stationery.
All of the following items are printed in the Golden type.

## D3
London County Council Scholarship Certificates On 16 January 1894 H.
P.
Harris wrote to Morris: ‘I have been asked, to ask you if the Kelmscott Press would undertake the printing of certificates for scholarship boys etc. for the Technical Instruction Committee of the London County Council.
There is a feeling that to have such things printed in the best possible way would be a good thing all round.
What do you say to it?
If you are disposed to take it up, I will let the proper authorities know'(cLMorrisiana 23’).
By the following autumn the Press received the first printing order.
Morris is said to have complained, ‘I am not a jobbing printer', but he agreed to the request because of his friendship with Dr William Garnett of the Technical Education Board (Colebrook, p.
37).
Cockerell wrote to Garnett on 26 October 1894: I beg to acknowledge, with thanks, your order of yesterday's date for 1ooo copies of the certificate, which shall be put in hand as soon as possible.
I regret that we are unable to supply envelopes'(LB).
And on 2o November Cockerell reported to Garnett: ‘The certificates have now been printed, & they will be sent to you today.
I hope that you will find them satisfactory'(LB).
Each of the certificates was printed in the Golden type, with one decorated initial, on a single leaf of Flower paper approximately 18o × 225 mm, using the frames designed by Morris for the illustrations in the Chaucer (A4o).
(However, two of the certificates – nos.
22 and 28 – include frames intended for the Chaucer but not printed in it.) Altogether 9942 copies of the certificates were printed.
The following list is based on a table supplied by Tomkinson (pp.
127-8), the details of whichare taken from the set of Certificates in Mr.
S.
C.
Cockerell's Collection'; an annotated set of certificates in the ‘Specimens' book; an incomplete annotated set in NYPL; entries in Proctor's notebook; and an analysis of the certificates in Proctor's hand (CL Morrisiana 23’).

## D4
Keats Memorial Leaflet ‘AN AMERICAN MEMORIAL TO KEATS.'(‘Specimens', no.
3o.) Golden type, three- and ten-line initials.
2 leaves (printed on outer side of first leaf only) of Flower paper, each 207 × 144 mm.
750 copies.
Ms in Sir Edmund Gosse's hand and corrected proof: CL Morrisiana 23’.
The occasion was the unveiling of a bust of Keats by Miss Anne Whitney of Boston at Hampstead Parish Church on 16 July 1894.
On 18 June, Gosse, who was to participate in the ceremony, wrote to Morris: I have just received from Mr Day, of the American Keats Memorial Committee, a note from Lyons asking me to send you the enclosed form of invitation, which he tells me you are going to print at the Kelmscott Press.
I have copied out his rough draft, & send you the form.
I presume that Mr Day has communicated fully on the subject with you already'(CL Morrisiana 23’).
In a cancelled version of the leaflet (Specimens', no.
44), it was announced that Bret Harte would represent the donors and that Dr Alexander C.
MacKenzie would perform on the organ: Cancelled, as neither Bret Harte, nor Dr Mackenzie were able to attend’(Cockerell's note) (CL‘Morrisiana 23’).
An accompanying invitation (‘Specimens’, no.
29) to the unveiling is in Golden type, with six-line initial.
2 leaves (printed on outer side of first leaf only) of Flower paper, each 102 × 144 mm.
75o copies.

## D5
Ancoats Brotherhood Leaflet ANCOATS BROTHERHOOD. / March, 1894, to / March 1895.' (Specimens', no.
17.) Golden type, six- and ten-line initials, side and corner borders, illustration by Burne-Jones.
Two leaves of Flower paper, each 204 × 143 mm.
Note by Cockerell: 25o copies, 1894'(CL Morrisiana 23’).
Proofs: HPL; CL ‘red portfolio'.
The frontispiece of John Ball (A6) appears on p.
1; an extract from Tennyson's ‘The Golden Year' appears on p.
2; a passage from Ruskin appears on p.
3; Tennyson's Flower in the Crannied Wall'and printer's mark I appear on p.
4.
The cost of production was £1o I3s.
(Ledger).
Morris met Charles Rowley in 1883, and, at Walker's suggestion, Rowley persuaded Morris to lecture to the Ancoats Brotherhood, a socialist workers’organization in Manchester; Morris frequently attended the Ancoats meetings thereafter (reminiscences by Rowley [BL Add.
Ms.
45347, ff.
93–6]).

## D6
Address to Sir Lowthian Bell ‘TO SIR LOWTHIAN BELL, BARONET, / F.R.S., D.C.L., &c., &c., THE FOUNDER OF/ THE FIRM OF BELL BROTHERS IN THE YEAR 1844 [two ornaments 3]'.
(Specimens', no.
27.) Golden type, six-line initial.
Four leaves of Flower paper, each 2o7 × 144 mm.
Proofs: BL C.43.c.26 (ΣFF 2o).
Note by Cockerell: 250 on paper, 2 on vellum 1894’(CL‘Morrisiana 23’).
The Address, dated 3o June 1894, appears on pp.
1-2; names of the officials of Bell Brothers appear on pp.
3-5; names ofthe Representatives appointed by the Workmen to make the presentation on their behalf' appear on pp.
6-7; p.
8 is blank.
The occasion was the fiftieth anniniversary of the founding of Bell Brothers by Sir Isaac Lowthian Bell (1816–19o4) and his two brothers, Thomas and John.
For the anniversary Walter Crane also designed a folio broadside, engraved by A.
Leverett, with a portrait of Sir Lowthian, a border, and a congratulatory poem, printed by Walker & Boutall on Batchelor paper (reproduced in Saks sale, no.
152).
The cost of production, not including the binding of the two vellum copies, was £3 16s.
3d.
(Ledger).

## D7
Deaconess Institution Associate's Card ‘The Deaconess Institution for / the Diocese of Rochester, 83 / North Side, Clapham Common. / Associates'Card.'(Specimens', no.
137.) Chaucer and Troy types, five-line initial, frame.
Two leaves of unusually thick Flower paper, each 127 × 79 mm.
Design for frame: CL.
Proofs: JC (corrected); CL red portfolio'.
Trial proofs: Sotheby cat., 18 December 1944, no.
194; CL ‘red portfolio’.
April 2.
1897.
250 & 10 copies.
Flower {paper]: 2 thicknesses.'(‘Specimens’).
The heading and rules appear on p.
I; a prayer (within a frame) appears on pp.
2-3; a scriptural text appears on p.
4.
The frame, not used elsewhere, was designed for the illustrations of a projected edition of The House of the Wolfings [B24]’ (List’).
Cockerell wrote to Jane Morris on 19 March 1897: ‘Mrs Gilmore [William Morris's sister Isabella, who was head of the Institution] was anxious to have the associate's card of her institution printed at the Kelmscott Press, and it is now being done.
Please let me know whether you wish her to be charged for it.
It is a small affair, and one that might be made an “unbirthday present" of, if you think well' (BL Add.
Ms.
52738, f.
11).

## D8
The Wayzegoose Menus The annual printers' dinners of the KP were held at the Royal Stag Hotel, Datchet, 5 September 1892; at the Crown Hotel, Broxbourne, 8 September 1893; and at the Dumb Bell Hotel, Taplow, 31 August 1894 and 13 September 1895.
Only the 1894 menu is represented in the ‘Specimens’book (no.
23); in a complete set of the menus (CL 'Morrisiana 23'; another set is in PML 76941) there is this note by Cockerell: ‘A very few copies of these four menus were printed.
The arrangement was that of the compositors and was not shown to Morris.'In each case the KP printers have introduced as many of the Kelmscott types and ornamênts as possible, though May Morris quotes one of the printers as complaining, . . . it isn't quite what we could have done if we'd had more “sorts"’ (Introductions, ii, 43s).
The menus are each two leaves: the venue appears on p.
I; the menu on p.
2; the programme on p.
3; and printer's mark 1 on p.
4.
The 1892 ānd 1893 menus are on card stock (each leaf 178× 114 mm and 234× 178 mm, respectively); the 1894 and 1895 menus are on Batchelor paper (each leaf 215 × 142 mm in both).
All are in two or more colours.

94 for Walker & Boutall'; ‘Not used’.
The label may have been abandoned because the spine of the volume was extremely narrow.
3.
When the kP was winding down its operations, Mrs.
Morris gave books from the remaining stock to a number of British libraries, each accompanied by a presentation label (see Dio.1o).
Most of these books were offered in a quarter holland binding, though they were originally issued in limp vellum at the time of publication; hence it became necessary to print new spine labels for the following books.
(Copies examined: BL C.43.26 [∆.d.1.].) ‘SIDONIA / THE / SORCER-ESS.'(Troy type). ‘GODEFREY / OF / BOLOYNE.' (Troy type). ‘THE GLIT-/TERING / PLAIN.
(Golden type). ‘THE WELL / AT THE / WORLD'S END / BY / WILLIAM / MORRIS' (Golden type).
A proof of the last label appears in the ‘Specimens’' book (no.
123) with this note: 5o [copies printed].
Oct.
31, 1896'.

## D12
Miscellaneous 1.
CHANGE OF ADDRESS. / [ornament 2] Mr.
Halliday Sparling, having been com-/pelled by the pressure of other work to resign / the Secretaryship to the Kelmscott Press, has / been succeeded by Mr.
S.
C.
Cockerell.
Cor-/respondents are asked to note the consequent / change of address.'(Specimens’, no.
28.) Golden type.
One leaf of Batchelor paper, 69 × 138 mm.
Inserted in the list of 2 July 1894 (see C23), on which date the change became effective.
Sparling resigned the Secretaryship because his marriage with Morris's daughter May had broken up.
2.
For errata and other slips inserted in the KP books, see A7, A32, A35, A42, and A53.
3.
According to Tomkinson (p.
126), An Address Delivered . . . at the Distribution of Prizes to Students of the Birmingham Municipal School of Art (1898), the first of six lectures by Morris printed by the Chiswick Press in the Golden type and published by Longmans, was set up at the Kelmscott Press just before it closed and the type was transferred to the Chiswick Press for printing, but a copy is in the possession of Mr.
S.
C.
Cockerell, printed at the Kelmscott Press before the type was transferred’.
4.‘WILLIAM MORRIS /POET CRAFTSMAN / SOCIAL-IST LIVED / HERE 1878-1896’.
(Copy examined: CL.) Golden type on Batchelor paper.
Note by Cockerell: ‘This is reproduced outside Kelmscott House' 5. ‘THIS BOOKCASE FORMERLY BE-/LONGED TO WILLIAM MORRIS [ornament 3]'.
(Copy examined: CL.) Golden type on Batchelor paper.
Note by Cockerell: ‘Done for the purchaser of one of w.M.'s bookcases'.
6.
IN MEMORY OF / JAMES LEIGH JOYNES / FOR-MERLY SCHOLAR OF / ETON COLLÈGE AND OF / KING'S COLLEGE CAMBRIDGE / BORN MAY 29th 1853 / DIED JANUARY 13th 1893 / UNTIL THE DAY BREAK AND / THE SHADOWS FLEE AWAY.'(Copy examined: CL.) Golden type on Batchelor paper.
Joynes was a former classical master at Eton who became associated with some of Morris's socialist activities.
7. ‘IN LOVING AND REV-/ERENT MEMORY OF / HENRIETTA JONES, / THE WIFE OF / WILLIAM JOHN JONES / (SYLVANUS DAUNCEY) / BORN JULY 12th, 1865, / DIED MAY 24th, 1896. / “BELOVED TILL LIFE / CAN CHARM NO MORE, AND MOURNED TILL/ PITY'S SELF BE DEAD."’(Copy examined: CL.) Golden type on Batchelor paper.
8.
IN MEMORY OF THOMAS SADLER, Ph.D., / BORN JULY 5, 1822.
DIED SEPT 1, 1891 [ornament 2] MINISTER FROM 1846, OF THE OLD CHAPEL / & FROM 1862 OF THE CONGREGATION MEETING HERE [ornament 2] FIVE TIMES / DURING A PASTORATE OF 45 YEARS HIS FOLD WAS ENLARGED FOR AN / INCREASING FLOCK, DRAWN TO HIM BY THE GRACES OF HIS APOSTOLIC / CHARACTER [ornament 2] BORN OF THE SPIRIT, HE SHARED THE BLESSING OF THE PURE / IN HEART [ornament 2] THE SECRET OF A LIFE HID WITH CHRIST IN GOD WAS FELT / IN THE SERVICES OF THIS HOUSE, & SEEN IN HIS PATIENCE OF FAITH, HOPE, & / LOVE [ornament 2] VERSED IN THE SCRIPTURES & THE RECORDS OF THÈ CHURCH, HE / LOOKED ON THEIR HUMAN TRAITS WITH HISTORIC SYMPATHY, & ON / THEIR LIGHTS OF DIVINE TRUTH AS INTO THE OPENED HEAVENS [ornament 2] / HE LOVED THE COMMUNION OF SAINTLY SOULS CHIEFLY OF THOSE / NEAREST TO THE SIMPLICITY OF CHRIST [two ornaments 2] IN HIS OWN HOME,/ SUDDENLY, SILENTLY, ALONE， HE PASSED WITHIN THE VEIL WHERE / ALL LIVE UNTO GOD.’(‘Specimens', no.
64.) Note by Cockerell: ‘Nov.
1894.
450 copies' (cL Morrisiana 23’).
Golden type.
One leaf of Flower paper, 123 × 253 mm.
Corrected proof: BL C.43.f.24 (∆b12).
A slip giving the text of a memorial to Dr.
Thomas Sadler, for distribution at the unveiling of it in Rosslyn Hill Chapel, Hampstead' (List’).
On 17 September 1894, Cockerell wrote to Morris & Co.: We can print the inscription by Thursday next, if you give the order by return of post.
The size of the paper will be determined by the length that the lines make, as we have only one size of Roman type.
The cost of 45o copies will be two guineas'(LB).
On 2o November he wrote again: ‘As the time was so short, & it might be inconvenient to print the inscription later in the week, I thought it best to put it in hand at once.
A few copies had been struck off when your telegram arrived, but the correction has now been made'(LB).
9.
Morris supplied specimen passages in the Golden, Chaucer, and Troy types, printed on one side only of a single leaf of Flower paper (‘Specimens', no.
75), 189 × 138 mm, to be included in Edward F.
Strange's Alphabets: A Handbook of Lettering with Historical, Critical and Practical Descriptions, Ex-Libris Series (London: George Bell and Sons, 1895), following p.
2o6.
Note by Cockerell: 2ooo like this and 6o on large paper.
Dec.
'94.’(CL ‘Morrisiana 23’).
On 7 December 1894 Strange wrote to Cockerell: ‘Thanks for the proof which I like very much.
I have sent it on to Messrs.
Bell & Sons from whom you will receive a formal order, with precise details as to size of paper &c'(íEH HM 36674).
To Gleeson White, the editor of the series, Cockerell # WILLIAM MORRIS.
DiedOctober3rd,1896.
HE idle singer of an empty day”: Thy story, poet, otherwisé we read, for lol thy day, too early passed away, Was full of lofty thought and knightly deed.
AST on an age of change, and stress, and strife, Thy aim was still, through manhood up from youth, To beautify the méanest things of life With that rich light that flows from Art and Truth.
ARTH'S soiledand motley pageant to thine eyes Was sad; thy dream was of a dawn; golden dawn; Chy quest was for an "Earthly Paradise"': H brighter waits thee now where thou art gone 23 A rare piece of Kelmscott Press ephemera (D12.11 – reduced): a poem written and printed by the Press workers on the occasion of Morris's death. Ônly one copy is known to be extant.
(Private collection of John J.
Walsdorf, Jr.) reported on 1o January 1895: ‘The price of 2ooo copies of the leaflet for Mr.
Stange's book, like sample, & 5o copies on à larger page, will be 5½ guineas.
To prevent a mistake in the latter, will you kindly send me a piece cut to the exact size required (including edge for turning in), when you give the order.
Perhaps it would be well to let me have a similar pattern for the ordinary page as well, with edge required by binders marked, as the amount of this will affect the position of the type'(LB).
White did not formally place the order until 2ı February 1895 (HEH HM 36675), and Cockerell sent him the leaves on 12 March (LB).
1o.
Kelmscott House stationery.
(Copies examined: BL Add.
Ms.
4534o.) ‘Kelmscott House, Upper Mall, /Hammersmith, W. /.
189 ’.
Golden type.
Two leaves of thin laid paper watermarked Silver Linen’, each 200 × 125 mm.
Morris had earlier (until about 1894) used Kelmscott House stationery printed in sans-serif type, probably not done at the KP.
11.
WILLIAM MORRIS. [two ornaments 3] / Died October 3rd, 1898, [four ornaments 3]’.
(Copy examined: wc.) A twelve-line poem in Chaucer type (first line of title in Troy type), with three- and seven-line initials and a verso octavo border, on one leaf of Batchelor paper, 244 × 184 mm.
On the verso is this pencilled note: ‘Printed at the Kelmscott Press on the death of William Morris by some of the workers there.
Chas.
MacLeish.
12.
Morris supplied a specimen leaf from The Golden Legend (A7), 283 × 215 mm on Batchelor paper, printed on the recto only, for the second edition of Joseph Pennell's Pen Drawing and Pen Draughtsmen: Their Work and Their Methods (London: Macmillan, 1894), following p.
4I4.
The page is headed ‘OF THADUENT OF OUR LORDE.’, is set in the Golden type, and contains a border and six- and ten-line initials.
Pennell noted that ‘at the moment of going to press [his preface is dated 4 April 1894] Mr.
William Morris has lent me a page of his type and decorations'(p.
414).
In the list of illustrations the page was described as ‘printed at a hand press on Kelmscott Press paper'(p. xxxvi).
A note by Cockerell indicates that the page was reset for the occasion (Saks sale, no.
181[15]).
13.
In Theodore Low De Vinne's The Practice of Typography . . .
Plain Printing Types (New York: Century, 1899), p.
207, there is a brief passage (recto only of a leaf) on type-design which is set in the Golden type with a ten-line initial.
A note by Cockerell (BL C.102.h.18) offers this explanation: Short account of the Press written by W.M. for one of De Vinne's books on Printing.
An electro was set to Mr De Vinne.' E.
CONTRACTS AND MEMORANDA FOR KELMSCOTT PRESS BOOKS

## E1
.
Contract for The Golden Legend (A7) (BL C.102.- h.18) London September IIth.
1890 Agreement between Mr.
Fred.
S.
Ellis of the Red House Chelston Torquay and Mr.
William Morris of Kelmscott House Hammersmith on the one side, and Mr.
Bernard Quaritch of 15 Piccadilly London. respecting a reprint of Caxton's Golden Legend.
1.
Messrs.
Ellis & Morris agree to re-edit the original edition of Caxton's Golden Legend free of charge.
2.
Each of the two Editors to receive on the completion of the reprint, twelve copies, – and if a Large Paper issue should be decided upon each editor three of them. [Added in margin: ‘gratis'.] 3.
Mr.
Morris to have absolute and sole control over choice of paper, choice of type, size of the reprint and selection of the printer[.] 4.
Mr.
Quaritch agrees in his name and that of his successors, executors, heirs or assigns to pay for the paper, printing and binding of the reprint. [signed] Bernard Quaritch F.
S.
Ellis William Morris [Additional handwritten memoranda on Quaritch's copy: ‘15 Dec.
1891.
500 copies to cost B.Q. £,125.00.
28 Mch 1892.
I agree to pay £100.- further towards corrections.
B.
Quaritch.'The negotiations that lay behind this contract can be traced in a series of letters: Quaritch to Ellis, 6 September 189o (BC); Morris to Ellis, 7 September (Mackail, ii, 263); Quaritch to Ellis, 8 September (BC); and Quaritch to Ellis, II September (BC).] E2 Contract for The Recuyell of the Historyes of Troye (A8) (HEH HM 36663) Agreement between Mr.
William Morris of Kelmscott House Hammersmith W and Mr.
Bernard Quaritch of 15 Piccadilly London W

## London
3 June 1892 Concerning the sale and purchase of Mr.
William Morris' reprint of Caxton's "The Recuyell of the Historyes of Troy’ I.
The edition to consist of 3oo copies on fine hand-made linen paper, and 5 copies on vellum.
2.
Mr.
Morris agrees to sell 275 of the above copies on paper, bound in vellum or morocco [sic] at Seven Pounds, (£7.) each, and two copies on vellum at Fifty Pounds (£so) each.
3.
Mr.
Morris engages not to sell any of his 25 copies on paper until Mr.
Quaritch has exhausted his stock.
4.
Mr.
Morris agrees to have the book printed and delivered bound, not later than the end of March 1893.
5.
Mr.
Morris stipulates that Mr.
Quaritch shall publish the paper copies at the Subscription price of Nine guineas (£9.9.o) but Mr.
Quaritch to be at liberty to close the subscription on the day of issue, and to put his own price on any copies left unsold at that time.
6.
Mr.
Quaritch, or his executors, agrees to purchase the 275 copies on paper, and two copies on vellum at the prices before mentioned, viz £7. each for copies on paper, and £so each for the copies on vellum.
7.
Payment to be made as follows; – One half of the amount due to be paid one week after delivery of the stock, and the second half three months after delivery of the stock.
8.
Mr.
Quaritch agrees to supply the 5 copies for the Public Libraries.
9.
Mr.
Quaritch undertakes to give preference to any orders given for the book to Mr.
Morris, should there be any difficulty about supplying copies. [signed] Bernard Quaritch William Morris

## E3
Proposal for The Story of the Glittering Plain (A22) (AL) Proposal for producing an illustrated Edition of the Glittering Plain The illustrations to be drawn by Walter Crane, & cut on wood by Mr Leverett or others to Walter Čranes satisfaction.
The book to be printed at the Kelmscott Press with William Morris' ornaments Any profits that may result after expenses have been paid to be divided equally between the said Walter Crane & William Morris [Undated, in Morris's hand.
In a letter to Crane of 24 September (?1892) (Yale), Morris wrote: ‘As to the Glittering Plain, I wil call on you tomorrow morning if I do not hear from you stopping me; I shall be very pleased to see your work and hope we may soon set about cutting some blocks.
I will write out a memorandum and bring it with me, as I quite agree with you that it is best to have these things explicit from the first.’]

## E4
Memorandum from Morris to Quaritch about Reynard the Foxe (A1o), 25 November 1892 (BQ) I propose to sell you 275 copies on paper of my edition of Reynard the Foxe, bound in limp vellum, at £2.2.- per copy – I retaining 25 copies for myself: 3oo copies in all to be printed.
Out of 1o copies as printed on vellum I propose to sell you 6 at £9.9.- per copy.
The payments for the above paper copies to be made one half $( \mathscr { L } ^ { 2 8 8 .
1 5 . - } )$ on delivery – the other half $\left( \mathcal { L } 2 8 8 . \mathrm { I } 5 . - \right)$ in three months time from the date of delivery.
The vellum copies to be paid for six months from delivery $( \mathcal { L } 5 6 .
1 4 ^ { - } )$ .
I engage to deliver the whole book before the Ist of March, 1893.
I make it a condition that you announce the approaching publication and receive subscribers' names for the book at once at the price of $\underline { { { \ f } } } _ { 3 } .
3 { \cdot } \overline { { { \cdot } } } \overline { { { \cdot } } } \overline { { { \cdot } } }$ but I leave you at liberty to close the subscription on receipt of the stock, and to raise the price after that time if you think good.

## Es
Memorandum from Morris to Macmillan & Co. about Tennyson's Maud (A17), 5 January 1893 (BL Add.
MS.
55260, ff.
119-20) My estimate for printing 5oo copies of Tennyson's Maud in black & red duly ornamented in the style of the Kelmscott Press, and delivered bound in limp vellum with silk ties is sixteen shillings per copy.
I stipulate also that I shall be allowed to print ten good copies extra for my private use, which however I bind myself not to sell till your edition is exhausted.
It is my custom to print a few copies of my books on vellum: if you should wish any copies so printed I can offer these at £s.5.o per copy.
I should print ten good copies, and propose to offer you eight, keeping two for myself on the same conditions as the other good ones.
You understand that this is for Maud'by itself, which will make a very small book; if you should wish to add something more to that poem, it would be better I think & the book would cost proportionately less. [Memorandum in another hand on verso: <table><tr><td>Maud Cost of soo –</td><td>423.
400 - -</td></tr><tr><td>Royalty 1o/- per c</td><td>250 - - 65o - -</td></tr><tr><td>485 sold @ 31/6</td><td>763.17.6</td></tr><tr><td>Profit Mem: £2oo to be paid to Lord Tennyson]</td><td>113.17.6</td></tr></table>

## E6
Memorandum from Ellis & Elvey to William Michael Rossetti about D.
G.
Rossetti's poems (A2o and A2oa), 4 July 1893 (uCLA: Ellis papers) We now have seen Mr.
Morris & have his estimate for printing the Rossetti yols. at the Kelmscott Press.
He suggests dividing the “Poetical Works" into 2 vols., the first containing the Ballads & Narrative poems as arranged by yourself, & the other the rest of the poems.
His price for printing 3oo copies is 21/- per vol including binding.
No we cannot publish it at a higher price than 42/- of which we must allow the trade 20%, viz 8/- to induce them to take it up.
This brings our 42/- to 34/- leaving us a gross profit of 18/- per vol.
If 35o are sold this will amount to £I95 on EACH of the 2 vols.
We will settle with Mr.
Morris & pay for the book & manage the sales, and an account shall be kept of cost & all expenses, & against that shall be set all the proceeds of the sales, & after all expenses have been paid the profit shall be divided from time to time between us, you taking one third and & we taking two thirds which will include our working expense.

## E7
Memorandum from Cockerell to Way and Williams about Rossetti’s Hand and Soul (A36), 26 June 1895 (LB).

## Hand
& Soul' I have now gone into the cost of producing the above in the style of Mr.
Morris’ lecture on Gothic Architecture, but bound in vellum (without ties), & the price for 5oo paper copies will be 5/- per copy, with a title designed by Mr.
Morris, & for sāy a dozen vellum copies, 15/- per copy.
In addition to the above numbers Mr.
Morris would reserve the right of printing 25 paper copies & 4 vellum copies for his personal use.
If you accept this estimate it will remain for you to fix the selling price, & we could then arrange terms for the sale of copies in England.
In writing will you kindly say how many copies you propose to leave for the English market (perhaps 2oo would be a suitable number, leaving 3oo for America) – & whether you will be prepared to pay for the whole issue within a month of delivery. [In a second letter to Way and Williams, 19 July 1895 (LB), Cockerell wrote as follows:] I beg to acknowledge, with thanks, your letter of the 6th inst. accepting Mr.
Morris' proposals for the printing ofHand & Soul’.
The price quoted does not include carriage to America, as we could only undertake to deliver the books to your agent in London.
As to selling price I think that it should not be more than 1o/- for the paper copies & 3o/- for the vellum copies, seeing how very small the book will be.
On the copies sold through our agency in London, Mr.
Morris would expect a discount of 25% to cover discount to trade, printing of announcement etc., thus leaving you a profit of 2/6 on each paper copy sold, if you accept the selling prices suggested above.
I do not think it will be necessary to refer to Messrs Ellis & Elvey, as the copyright has now elapsed.
With regard to payment, I meant within a month after the issue of the book.
If you will say how many vellum copies you are prepared to take, the number will thus depend on how many we can see our way to dispose of over here.
Until the book is actually in the press, it would be well not to make any definite announcement to the public as to date of issue, price, number etc. though I think you may rely on having the book by November. ...
P.s.
Mr.
Morris is afraid that he cannot undertake to print a larger book for you at present, as he has so many projects of his own which will occupy the press for some time. [Final arrangements were made in a third letter from Cockerell to Way and Williams, 21 August 1895 (LB):] I beg to acknowledge your favours of the sth & 8th inst., agreeing to Mr.
Morris' terms for the production of Hand & Soul’, & fixing the number of vellum copies at 15, in addition to the copies for Mr.
Morris' personal use.
The price does not include the cost of shipment to America, or of announcements for America.
The 2oo copies for England Mr.
Morris will take off your hands, paying you a premium of 2/6, & I shall be pleased to send you a list of our American buyers in order that you may deal with them direct.
The book will be printed in red & black, & the binding will be stiff vellum (i.e. with a thin board), which will overcome the disadvantage to which you refer.
I am sorry that Mr.
Morris does not see his way to supplying you with a specimen for your booklet.

## E8
Memorandum from Cockerell to Theodore Watts about the latter's poems (B4), 20 October 1896 (HEH HM 36683) Since I saw you yesterday, I have gone more thoroughly into the question of the proposed volume of your poems, and I think that it would lead to confusion if the books were sold by us as well as by Mr.
Lane, so I should propose his taking the whole issue – my transferring to him the orders we have received, as was done when Rossetti's Poems were published by Ellis & Elvey.
We have on our books orders for 9o copies, a few of which however may eventually be withdrawn.
For a volume of the size of the Defence of Guenevere, $\left( \mathrm { { { r 7 o } } \ p p } \right)$ printed in red & black, & bound in limp vellum with silk ties, I can quote 2oo copies for $\mathcal { E } _ { } { 2 } \varsigma _ { 0 }$ ,225 for $\mathscr { E } , 2 7 5$ or 25o for $\mathcal { E } 3 0 0$ : payment to be one half when the book goes to the binder, & the balance on delivery.
If Mr.
Lane agrees to these terms I shall be glad to hear from him & (?or) to receive the copy, arranged & finally corrected for printing, as soon as possible. [Postscript] We would print a few copies on vellum at 6 guineas, to be sold at 8 guineas.
I should reserve the right to print ten copies on paper & 2 vellum for Mrs.
Morris to give away. # INDEX OF COLLECTIONS This index provides a record of the private and public collections drawn upon in compiling this bibliography.

(yc), 69(r), 82(1), 134(f) # INDEX OF ASSOCIATION COPIES OF KELMSCOTT BOOKS These items are broken down into three categories: books presented to a person (τo); books presented by a person (FRom); and books owned by a person (owNED).
Since most of the books were presented by Morris to his friends, the second category is not included under his name.
On the other hand, Kelmscott books that were added to his library at Kelmscott House after his death and in which his bookplate appears are listed as his copies (OWNED).
Italic letters in parentheses refer to entries in the RELATED MATERIALS sections.
